Mocking a Circuit Breaker: 503 While Open, Then a Half-Open Recovery

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 4 • Originally published at powerduck.com

Quick answer: A circuit breaker is a state machine with three states: closed, open, and half-open. When the downstream dependency fails enough times, the breaker opens and the client gets a fast failure without hammering the broken service. After a cool-down it moves to half-open and lets a trial request through. To test client behavior honestly, mock the full sequence rather than a single static 503, because the interesting logic is how the client recovers, not just how it fails.

Most breaker implementations, from Resilience4j to Polly and the Go-style hand-rolled versions, share the same model. The difference between them is mostly configuration and naming.

If your mock always returns 503 Service Unavailable, you can verify that the client shows an error, but you cannot test the two behaviors that matter most: that the client stops firing requests while the breaker is open, and that it recovers automatically once the dependency is healthy. A good mock advances time and state so the test can observe the transitions.

Here is a dependency-free Node mock that tracks failure counts and exposes the state transitions. The threshold opens the breaker, and a simulated wait moves it to half-open.

function createDownstream() { const FAILURE_THRESHOLD = 3; let failures = 0; let state = 'CLOSED'; let openedAt = 0; const COOLDOWN_MS = 5000; async function call(now) { if (state === 'OPEN') { if (now - openedAt >= COOLDOWN_MS) state = 'HALF_OPEN'; else return { status: 503, fast: true }; } const healthy = serviceHealthy; // flip this in your test if (healthy) { failures = 0; state = 'CLOSED'; return { status: 200 }; } failures += 1; if (state === 'HALF_OPEN' || failures >= FAILURE_THRESHOLD) { state = 'OPEN'; openedAt = now; } return { status: 503, fast: state === 'OPEN' }; } return { call, getState: () => state }; }

Use fake timers so the cool-down does not slow the test, and assert on the state, not just the returned status.

test('opens after threshold, then recovers through half-open', async () => { jest.useFakeTimers(); const downstream = createDownstream(); serviceHealthy = false; for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) await downstream.call(Date.now()); expect(downstream.getState()).toBe('OPEN'); // While open, the next call fails fast and does not touch the service. const fast = await downstream.call(Date.now()); expect(fast).toMatchObject({ status: 503, fast: true }); // After the cool-down, a trial request probes recovery. jest.advanceTimersByTime(5000); serviceHealthy = true; const trial = await downstream.call(Date.now()); expect(trial).toEqual({ status: 200 }); expect(downstream.getState()).toBe('CLOSED'); });

One subtle point worth mocking explicitly: a 503 returned while the breaker is open is a local, instant decision, whereas a 503 during the closed state came from the actual service. The client can treat them differently, for example by skipping retry on an open breaker but retrying a genuine upstream failure. If your mock returns the same body for both, the client cannot distinguish them and you lose the ability to test the retry policy. Add a flag or a distinct error type so the branch is observable.

If the downstream is described in OpenAPI, the 503 response and any Retry-After header should be part of the operation's documented responses. A spec-driven mock can generate the status code from that contract while your test supplies the state machine, which keeps the response shape aligned with what the real service returns. This is more reliable than hand-writing a response body that drifts from the contract over time.

A retry repeats a single failed request after a short delay. A circuit breaker tracks failures across many requests and short-circuits traffic when the dependency is clearly down, then probes recovery. They work together: retries handle a transient blip, while the breaker prevents retries from overwhelming a service that is already failing.

Mock the downstream responses and let the real breaker run against them, so you test the actual state machine and configuration. Mocking the breaker directly only verifies that your code calls it, not that the thresholds and cool-downs behave correctly.

HTTP services commonly use 503 Service Unavailable, often with a Retry-After header. Inside a single process, the breaker may reject with a named error instead. Match whatever the real boundary returns so the client handles it correctly.

There is no universal number. A low threshold reacts fast but can open on a brief blip; a high threshold is slower but more tolerant. Base it on the dependency's typical failure pattern and test both the threshold and the half-open recovery rather than guessing.

This is part of the The API Mocking Guide — start there for stateful patterns that model how real services fail and recover.

Why Your Image Previews Should Never Inherit the Original File's Metadata

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A user uploads a photograph, your application generates a smaller preview, and everything appears to work perfectly.

The image loads quickly. The dimensions are correct. The thumbnail looks sharp.

But there's a problem: the preview may still contain metadata from the original photograph, including GPS coordinates, camera details, timestamps, and information about the software used to edit it.

For applications that handle private photographs, client assets, or user-generated media, this is an easy detail to overlook.

The solution isn't simply to resize images. It's to treat preview generation as a separate data-processing boundary.

Photographs can contain several types of metadata.

IPTC and XMP metadata can contain additional descriptive information, copyright details, and application-specific properties.

Not every image includes these fields, but applications should never assume that an uploaded photograph contains only visible pixels.

Consider a private client gallery where someone uploads photographs taken at home.

If GPS coordinates survive into a publicly accessible preview, the application may accidentally expose location information that was never intended to be shared.

The image can look completely harmless while carrying sensitive information.

A common mistake is assuming that reducing the dimensions of an image removes its metadata.

That depends entirely on the processing library and how the output file is generated.

Some image-processing workflows preserve metadata by default. Others remove it unless explicitly configured to retain it.

Copying an uploaded file, changing its filename, or applying an optimization step is not sufficient evidence that metadata has been removed.

A reliable media pipeline should explicitly define which metadata is allowed in each output.

For most browser-facing previews, the safest default is simple:

Preserve the visual content, but discard unnecessary source metadata.

One approach is to decode an image into pixels and create a new output through Canvas.

This code decodes the uploaded image, scales it down, and encodes a new JPEG from the rendered pixels.

Unlike copying the original file, this process doesn't intentionally transfer the source EXIF, IPTC, or XMP metadata.

The browser may still generate new metadata in the resulting file, so verify the actual output rather than assuming it contains no metadata whatsoever.

Many photographs rely on EXIF orientation information to display correctly.

A photograph taken vertically on a smartphone may contain pixel data stored in a different orientation, together with metadata telling software how to rotate it.

If you remove that metadata without applying the rotation, the resulting preview can appear sideways.

The imageOrientation: "from-image" option tells supporting browsers to account for the orientation when creating the bitmap.

Once the corrected pixels are drawn onto Canvas, the newly encoded image no longer needs the original orientation instructions.

This is why metadata removal should happen as part of a deliberate image transformation, not as an unrelated cleanup operation.

Browser-generated previews can improve responsiveness, but they shouldn't become your application's only security boundary.

A modified client could skip preview processing entirely and upload the original file.

For a trusted preview pipeline, the server should independently validate incoming media and generate derivatives using a controlled image-processing environment.

A reasonable architecture looks like this:

This separation gives you much better control over what each audience receives.

It also means original files can remain available for authorized downloads without requiring the application to expose them during ordinary browsing.

For a broader perspective on photo delivery workflows and the practical challenges surrounding online galleries, the photography workflow guides provide additional context.

Metadata isn't the only concern when generating previews.

A compressed image can be surprisingly expensive once decoded.

For example, a 6000 × 4000 image requires approximately 96 MB just for an uncompressed four-channel pixel buffer.

Additional buffers, resizing operations, and concurrent processing can increase memory usage significantly.

In the browser, processing several large photographs simultaneously can freeze the interface or crash a mobile tab.

Use bounded concurrency and release resources as soon as they're no longer needed.

The earlier example calls bitmap.close() inside a finally block for this reason.

For large upload queues, consider processing previews sequentially or with a small concurrency limit.

A preview-processing pipeline needs more than visual tests.

Create test images containing known metadata values:

Process those images through the same pipeline used in production.

Then inspect the generated files with a metadata inspection tool.

The important question isn't whether your code includes a metadata-removal operation.

It's whether sensitive information actually survives in the delivered output.

Also test malformed files, unsupported formats, very large dimensions, and images with unusual orientation values.

Image optimization is often discussed in terms of compression ratios, loading speed, and visual quality.

Those things matter, but they don't tell the whole story.

A preview is a new asset with its own purpose, audience, and security requirements.

Treating it as a separate output rather than a smaller copy of the original makes it easier to control privacy, performance, caching, and access permissions.

The practical rule is straightforward: decode deliberately, generate clean derivatives, retain originals only where necessary, and verify what your application actually delivers.

Good media handling isn't just about displaying images correctly. It's also about understanding which information should never leave the processing pipeline.

South Korea orders massive crypto wallet lockdown following the Binance hack

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The FSC also requested stronger transfer monitoring, response staffing and reserves, while exchange implementation and any new custody threshold remain unspecified.

South Korea's Financial Services Commission has asked domestic crypto exchanges to keep a larger share of customer assets offline and strengthen their response to suspicious activity after the Binance hack.

The FSC urged operators to increase the proportion held in cold wallets, continuously monitor deposits and withdrawals, secure additional response staff, promptly report and share abnormal access attempts, and build additional reserves.

Together, the requests address how exchanges hold customer crypto, monitor transfers, respond to suspicious access, and maintain financial protection against incidents.

The regulator's Nov. 3 clarification said it immediately requested the measures at a meeting held at 10 a.m. on Nov. 2. Participants included the Financial Supervisory Service, Financial Security Institute, a crypto industry association, and major virtual-asset businesses. The FSC dates the Binance hack that prompted the review to Oct. 30.

The November meeting also examined controls over access to computer systems, approval and signing arrangements for wallet withdrawals, and administrator privileges. Those checks extend the review beyond the share of assets held offline to the systems and permissions used to move them.

South Korea's four requests for safer crypto custody

The FSC's Nov. 2 requests, reaffirmed Nov. 3, build on a custody framework adopted in 2024.

The four requests cover custody, transfers, incident response and financial buffers.

The releases do not report exchange-by-exchange implementation. The 80% ratio is the 2024 standard, not a newly announced threshold.

Method: Source-bound qualitative safeguard map. Connectors identify the common subject of requested protections, not transfers of funds, execution order or confirmed implementation. Selectable views distinguish historical rules from current requests. No derived figures.

Additional requests following the Binance hackThe FSC's July 2024 supervision-regulation notice set the custody share at 80% of customer crypto's defined economic value. The notice accompanied the legal framework taking effect on July 19, 2024.The ratio uses a defined valuation method based on quantities and average won conversion values over the preceding year. The calculation multiplies each type's quantity by its preceding-year average won conversion value, then adds the amounts.That gives the custody ratio a valuation basis across the different crypto assets held for customers, and also establishes standards for insurance, mutual-aid coverage, or reserves to meet liabilities arising from incidents such as hacking and computer failures.On Oct. 28, regulators began on-site inspections after Bithumb reported signs of an employee-information leak. The Nov. 2 meeting concerns the subsequent response to Binance and the safeguards sought across domestic operators.

The FSC's July 2024 supervision-regulation notice set the custody share at 80% of customer crypto's defined economic value. The notice accompanied the legal framework taking effect on July 19, 2024.

The ratio uses a defined valuation method based on quantities and average won conversion values over the preceding year. The calculation multiplies each type's quantity by its preceding-year average won conversion value, then adds the amounts.

That gives the custody ratio a valuation basis across the different crypto assets held for customers, and also establishes standards for insurance, mutual-aid coverage, or reserves to meet liabilities arising from incidents such as hacking and computer failures.

On Oct. 28, regulators began on-site inspections after Bithumb reported signs of an employee-information leak. The Nov. 2 meeting concerns the subsequent response to Binance and the safeguards sought across domestic operators.

Milan, domani turno di riposo per Gonçalo Ramos? Camarda scalpita...

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Come è accaduto anche nelle altre partite di Europa League, domani contro il Ferencvaros Ruben Amorim farà un ampio turnover e darà un po' di riposo a diversi titolarissimi. Tra questi potrebbe esserci anche Gonçalo Ramos, il quale, dopo aver saltato la trasferta di Bournemouth, ha giocato tutte le gare successive. Presupponendo la sua titolarità domenica contro il Genoa in campionato, è logico pensare che contro gli ungheresi potrebbe anche iniziare dalla panchina.

A scriverlo è questa mattina il Corriere dello Sport che spiega che per sostituirlo scalpita Francesco Camarda, il quale sta trovando poco spazio in questo avvio di stagione e quando è stato chiamato in causa non ha mai convinto. Giocare a San Siro davanti ai tifosi milanisti ch lo osannano ogni volta che scende in campo potrebbe essere la medicina migliore per aiutarlo a sbloccarsi e ad uscire da questo momento non semplice.

Your Agent's Context Fills Up Before You Type: What to Trim First

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Most advice about coding agents is about what you type. But by the time you type your first prompt, the agent's context window is already partly full: instruction files, memory, tool names, skill descriptions. Then every file it reads, every test run and every long log adds more, and all of it is re-sent with every request.

That matters for more than your bill. In Effective context engineering for AI agents, Anthropic describes context rot: as the number of tokens in the window grows, the model's ability to accurately recall information from it goes down. Their framing is that a model has an "attention budget", and every token you add spends some of it. The guiding principle they give is to "find the smallest set of high-signal tokens that maximize the likelihood of your desired outcome."

I covered the money side in Where Your Coding Agent's Token Bill Goes. This one is about quality: what fills the context, and what to trim first so the agent has room to think about your actual task. The examples use Claude Code because its docs spell out exactly what loads and when, but the order works for any agent.

Don't guess. In Claude Code, run /context. The context window docs describe it as a live breakdown by category, with optimization suggestions, including which CLAUDE.md and auto memory files loaded.

According to the same page, this is what loads before you type anything:

Write down the three biggest categories. That's your trim list, in order. If you don't use Claude Code, the question is the same: what does your agent load at startup, and which of those things did you actually need today?

Anthropic calls bloated tool sets "one of the most common failure modes": too much functionality, or tools that overlap so the model can't tell which one to use. Their test is a good one to borrow: if a human engineer can't say for sure which tool fits a situation, the agent can't be expected to do better.

Claude Code already softens the cost here. Per its MCP docs, tool search is on by default, so only tool names and server instructions enter context at session start; full definitions load when Claude actually needs a tool. That doesn't make every connected server free, though, and it doesn't fix overlapping tools. Three things still help:

If you write your own MCP server, the docs suggest keeping descriptions and server instructions concise and putting the important words near the start, since that's the part tool search reads.

Instruction files load every session, whether the task needs them or not. Claude Code's memory docs recommend keeping each CLAUDE.md under 200 lines, and note that imports help you organise a long file but don't reduce its context cost, because imported files load with it.

The fix is to move instructions to where they're needed:

I went through the full cut-and-move process in Your CLAUDE.md Is Too Long, so I won't repeat it here. One addition that's easy to miss is in Step 5 below: path-scoped rules don't survive compaction the way root instructions do.

Then look at what the agent reads during work. Vague prompts trigger broad scanning; the cost docs contrast "improve this codebase" with "add input validation to the login function in auth.ts". The second one lets the agent find what it needs with far fewer file reads.

Test runs, log files and documentation pages are the quiet context killers. A failing test suite can print thousands of lines when you needed ten.

Anthropic's post lists the same idea from the other direction: once a tool result is deep in history, the agent rarely needs to see the raw output again, and clearing old tool results is one of the lightest forms of compaction.

The longest-lived thing in your context is the conversation itself. You have two tools for it, and they do different things.

/clear when the task changes. Old conversation crowds out the files you need next and is re-sent with every message. The docs point out that /clear costs nothing, while /compact is itself a large request because it has to read the whole conversation it summarises. If you don't need continuity, clear. Use /rename first if you want to /resume that session later.

/compact with a focus when you do need continuity. Claude Code compacts automatically near the limit, but the automatic pass guesses what matters. Telling it is better:

You can also put standing instructions in your project's CLAUDE.md, as the docs show:

Before relying on compaction, know what comes back. Per the context window docs, after compaction:

So if a rule must hold for the whole session, don't scope it to paths; put it in the root file. And put the most important instructions at the top of each skill.

If you're building your own agent rather than using Claude Code, the same three techniques from Anthropic's post apply: compaction, structured note-taking (the agent writes notes to a file outside the window and reads them back later), and subagents with clean context windows.

These five steps are one slice of a bigger discipline. Context Engineering: The Complete Guide to the Skill That Replaced Prompt Engineering covers it in 40 pages: the anatomy of a context window, the four core strategies (Write, Select, Compress, Isolate) in depth, the four failure modes (poisoning, distraction, confusion and clash) with a diagnostic toolkit, context engineering in Claude Code and Cursor, 20 reusable patterns, 12 common pitfalls with fixes, and a 30-day plan.

How do you stop bots from deciding "Vim vs Emacs"? Building a one-human-one-vote poll

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every "which tool is better" poll on the internet has the same problem: the result says more about who brought the most bots and alt accounts than about what people actually use.

So I built Pollreal: 2–4 candidates, 24 hours, and every vote is one verified human.

I wrote down what's in place and what I haven't settled — brigades vs. real fans, bandwagon effects, the ranking method: FAIRNESS.md. I'd love for people to poke holes in it.

Closed polls so far include VS Code vs Vim vs Neovim vs Emacs and PostgreSQL vs MySQL vs MongoDB vs SQLite. Results can be read without signing up.

The logging setting that slows down new connections

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 4 • Edited on Nov 4 • Originally published at guardioz.com

The name log_hostname suggests that this setting puts the client address into PostgreSQL's server log. It does not: the address comes from %h in log_line_prefix. What log_hostname adds is a DNS lookup at the start of every new connection. We explain how the two settings work together, and we show what that lookup cost on PostgreSQL 18 when DNS was healthy, slow and down.

Three lines from one connection in our test. The log line prefix ends with %h, and the circled address is what %h prints: the IP address of the client that connected, not of the server. PostgreSQL logged it without any DNS lookup.

The server log can show where each connection came from. Two settings decide how, and they do different jobs.

The PostgreSQL 18 documentation describes log_hostname like this: "By default, connection log messages only show the IP address of the connecting host. Turning this parameter on causes logging of the host name as well. Note that depending on your host name resolution setup this might impose a non-negligible performance penalty."

Two details matter in practice. First, log_hostname adds no field of its own. Without %h - or %r, which adds the port - in log_line_prefix, ordinary log lines do not show the client at all, whatever log_hostname says, and the upstream default prefix, '%m [%p] ', has neither. The setting also changes the host= field of the "connection received" message, which appears when connection logging is on, and it fills the client_hostname column of pg_stat_activity. Second, in our tests the host name replaced the IP address in the log. It did not appear next to it.

A reverse DNS lookup asks DNS which name belongs to an IP address (a PTR record). With log_hostname on, PostgreSQL makes this lookup for every new TCP connection, right after the server process for that connection starts - before the client's first message is read and before authentication. The connection waits until DNS answers, or until the system's resolver gives up. Connections over a local Unix socket make no lookup.

The lookup runs whenever the setting is on, also when nothing in your configuration prints the name. Its time does not count against authentication_timeout, either. A comment next to the lookup in the PostgreSQL 18 source code says so directly: "PreAuthDelay and any DNS interactions above don't count against the time limit."

We measured the cost on two virtual machines in Microsoft Azure, in one private network: PostgreSQL 18.6 on Ubuntu 24.04 (Standard_D4s_v5, 4 vCPUs) and a separate client machine (Standard_D2s_v5). Connections used TLS 1.3 and SCRAM-SHA-256 password authentication, as many production setups do. The client ran pgbench, the benchmark tool that ships with PostgreSQL, with a one-line custom script (SELECT 1) and the -C option, which opens a new connection for every transaction. That makes connection setup most of the work, which is what this test is about. It is not a throughput test of a real workload.

Azure's DNS service answered the lookup for the client's address with the name vm-logtalk-hostname-cli.internal.cloudapp.net. Ubuntu runs a local DNS cache by default (systemd-resolved). Without such a cache, every lookup goes to the DNS server, so we tested the database server both ways.

For each setting, the table shows two numbers that pgbench prints at the end of a run. Connection time is how long it took, on average, to open one connection. Connections per second is how many times per second the client connected, ran SELECT 1 and disconnected; pgbench reports it as transactions per second, and with -C every transaction is one connection. With one client, each connection starts only after the previous one has finished, so the two numbers move in opposite directions: when every connection takes longer, fewer of them fit into a second. Connections per second here is the pace of a single client connecting again and again, not the most the server could accept.

Median of three 30-second pgbench runs per setting, run in alternating order: one client, a new connection for every transaction (pgbench -C), PostgreSQL 18.6 on Azure, Azure DNS answering normally. Connection time is pgbench's "average connection time"; the range shows the lowest and the highest of the three runs. Connections per second is pgbench's "tps (including reconnection times)".

Without a local cache, every new connection took about 6 ms longer: 43% more time per connection, and 29% fewer connections per second in this test. The server's own timing agrees. PostgreSQL 18 can log how long each connection took to set up (log_connections with the setup_durations option): the average grew from 10.4 ms to 16.3 ms, while the authentication part stayed at 6.0 ms in all three variants. The extra time is spent before authentication, where the lookup happens.

With the local cache, the difference was 0.3 ms. Azure DNS gave this answer a lifetime (TTL) of 10 seconds, so most lookups came from the cache. The 6 ms is what one round trip to Azure DNS cost from this server. On your network it is a different number: the time your DNS server needs to answer.

Healthy DNS is the best case. We then slowed DNS down, and then made it unreachable - only for the database server and only for DNS traffic - and ran the same test with one client.

One run per scenario: 30 seconds, 60 seconds for the run with DNS down and log_hostname on. One client, a new connection for every transaction, no local DNS cache unless the row says so. "DNS does not answer" means that the DNS server's answers never reached the database server, as in an outage. Connection time is pgbench's "average connection time"; in each run, the server's timing of the individual connections stayed close to this value.

With log_hostname off, DNS problems made no difference, because there was no lookup. With it on, every new connection waited for DNS. When DNS answered 100 ms late, each connection took about 109 ms longer. About 106 ms of that was the lookup itself: the 100 ms we added, on top of the 6 ms a lookup cost with healthy DNS. The remaining 2-3 ms were spread over the rest of the connection setup, and one run is not enough to explain them. When DNS did not answer, each connection waited until the resolver gave up: 20 seconds when the system resolver (glibc 2.39 on Ubuntu 24.04) asked DNS directly - it sent the question four times, five seconds apart - and 10 seconds through systemd-resolved. Resolver options can change these numbers, but not the pattern: a new connection takes as long as DNS takes to answer or to fail. With eight clients connecting at the same time and DNS down, the server completed 0.2 new connections per second. With log_hostname off and DNS healthy, the same eight clients completed 160 per second.

These are three lines from one of those 20-second connections, with %h in the log line prefix. The timestamp and process ID at the start of each line are replaced with ..., and long lines are wrapped:

When the lookup fails, PostgreSQL logs a WARNING and writes ??? in place of the client's host and port. The IP address the server already had does not appear anywhere in the log. It stays visible only in pg_stat_activity.client_addr, and only while the session is open. The last line also shows where the time went: 20 seconds of setup, of which authentication took 5.6 ms.

The cost does not depend on what you log. In one run we used the upstream default log_line_prefix and turned connection logging off, so nothing in the log could show a host name. Each connection still waited 20 seconds, and the only trace in the log was one WARNING line per connection.

With log_hostname on, PostgreSQL logs the name from the PTR record as it receives it. For the log, it does not check whether that name leads back to the same IP address. A PTR record belongs to the IP address, not to the domain in the name: it is set by whoever manages the reverse DNS for that address, usually the owner of the network the client connects from, or that network's provider. The owner of the domain in the name has no say in it. So whoever controls the reverse DNS of the client's address decides which name your log shows - even a name that looks like a different IP address.

We checked this on PostgreSQL 18.6 with a small DNS server that we controlled. We set the PTR record of the client address 127.0.0.5 to 10.0.0.5, and the PTR record of 127.0.0.6 to db-backup-01.corp.example, a name with no address behind it. These are five log lines from three connections. The log line prefix ends with %h; the timestamp and process ID are replaced with ..., and long lines are wrapped:

Every prefix and the "connection received" message show the name from the PTR record. In this test the real address appeared only in the text of a FATAL message, which PostgreSQL writes when it refuses a connection, and in pg_stat_activity.client_addr while a session was open.

PostgreSQL itself does not trust such a name for access control. A host name in pg_hba.conf matches only when the name also resolves back to the client's address, as the PostgreSQL 18 documentation describes. That is why the last connection was refused: our pg_hba.conf line for db-backup-01.corp.example did not match, because the name had no address. The log prefix showed the name anyway.

For an investigation, this means that a host name in the log is what the reverse DNS said, not proof of where a connection came from. With log_hostname off, %h logs the IP address the connection came from. If you need a name later, look the address up, and check that the name resolves back to the same address - the check that pg_hba.conf makes and the log does not.

The lookup happens once per connection, not once per query. In one 30-second run per setting with eight connections that stayed open, the server answered 19,291 queries per second with log_hostname off and 19,165 with it on. One run per setting cannot show whether a 0.7% difference is real, but the mechanism explains why it should be small: once a connection exists, it makes no more lookups.

This is why a connection pool can hide the setting for a long time. The cost comes back when the pool opens new connections - for example after a database restart or a failover, when every connection in the pool is opened again at about the same time, and each one makes its own lookup.

Connect to your database with psql or any other SQL client you normally use, and run:

To turn it off - in an SQL session, the same way as above, connected as a superuser:

The change needs a reload, not a restart, and applies to connections opened after the reload. To get the client address on every log line, add %h to log_line_prefix after %q. %q itself prints nothing: it tells background processes, which have no client, to stop at that point of the prefix. For example, in an SQL session as a superuser:

This prefix only demonstrates where %h goes; it is not a recommendation for your whole prefix. Keep the prefix you have and add %h to it, and update every tool that reads your log - log analyzers, alert rules - so it knows the new format.

To confirm the result, open a new connection and check that its log lines show the IP address in the prefix. On PostgreSQL 18 you can also measure connection setup on your own server: when log_connections includes setup_durations, the server logs "connection ready: setup total=..." for every new connection. That is one more log line per connection, so use it for a measurement window, not forever, on servers that open many connections.

On managed services, PostgreSQL settings live in the provider's console - a parameter group on Amazon RDS, server parameters on Azure Database for PostgreSQL, database flags on Google Cloud SQL - and the provider decides which of them you can change. Check what your server actually runs with the query above.

Sometimes a name is useful - for example, when client IP addresses change often and you keep logs for a long time. In that case, resolve the addresses when you read the log, not while clients wait to connect: an IP address in the log can be looked up later, during an investigation. Keep in mind that the answer then reflects DNS at the time you ask, not at the time of the connection.

Two more notes. Behind a connection pooler, %h shows the pooler's address, whatever log_hostname says. And to see which application a log line belongs to, use application_name: the client sets it, %a prints it, and it needs no lookup.

Two Azure virtual machines in the Poland Central region, in their own private network: the database server on Standard_D4s_v5 (Ubuntu 24.04, glibc 2.39, PostgreSQL 18.6 from the PostgreSQL project's packages) and the client on Standard_D2s_v5. PostgreSQL settings as installed by the packages, apart from the log line prefix, connection logging, log file handling and the listen address. Client-side numbers come from pgbench 18.6; server-side numbers from the PostgreSQL 18 log message "connection ready: setup total=..." of the benchmark's own connections. Slow DNS: 100 ms of delay added to every packet from the database server to Azure DNS (tc netem). DNS down: Azure DNS answers dropped at the database server's firewall (iptables), so the questions left and no answer came back. The healthy-DNS variants ran three times each, in alternating order; the slow, failing, eight-client and persistent-connection scenarios ran once each. The local DNS cache was emptied before every run. The test of unchecked names ran separately, on one Linux machine: PostgreSQL 18.6 and a dnsmasq DNS server in an isolated network namespace, with the system resolver set to use DNS only and clients connecting from 127.0.0.5 and 127.0.0.6.

The cases from this article in one picture. Times are from our test: pgbench -C, no local DNS cache.

Napoli-Lazio, tifosi ospiti verso lo stop: atteso il decreto della Prefettura

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il divieto dovrebbe riguardare anche gli altri settori del Maradona per chi risiede nella Regione Lazio.

Il settore ospiti del Maradona dovrebbe restare chiuso per Napoli-Lazio, in programma domenica 8 novembre alle 12:30. Secondo quanto riportato da Radio Laziale, la trasferta sarà vietata ai sostenitori biancocelesti. La decisione non è ancora ufficiale: si attende il decreto della Prefettura di Napoli, previsto tra oggi e domani.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il provvedimento dovrebbe impedire ai residenti nella Regione Lazio di acquistare biglietti per la partita. La restrizione non riguarderebbe soltanto il settore ospiti, ma anche gli altri settori dello stadio Diego Armando Maradona. Per i tifosi la notizia arriva dopo settimane segnate da una forte partecipazione alle trasferte della squadra.

Nei giorni scorsi l’Osservatorio Nazionale sulle Manifestazioni Sportive (ONMS) aveva rinviato la valutazione della gara al Comitato di Analisi per la Sicurezza delle Manifestazioni Sportive (CASMS). L’Osservatorio aveva inoltre invitato a non avviare la vendita dei biglietti destinati ai sostenitori ospiti.

Il quadro, secondo le indiscrezioni, sarebbe ora orientato verso la conferma del divieto e la chiusura del settore riservato ai tifosi della Lazio. Resta da attendere il decreto della Prefettura di Napoli: sarà il provvedimento ufficiale a formalizzare le restrizioni e a definirne le modalità di applicazione.

Se l’orientamento sarà confermato, la Lazio affronterà la trasferta senza i propri sostenitori provenienti dal territorio laziale. La limitazione all’acquisto dei biglietti, così come prospettata, si estenderebbe anche ai posti fuori dal settore ospiti.

Il possibile stop arriverebbe dopo una serie di partite lontano dall’Olimpico seguite da migliaia di sostenitori biancocelesti. A Reggio Emilia, in particolare, circa 10mila tifosi della Lazio hanno accompagnato la squadra, con una presenza significativa sugli spalti.

La partecipazione delle ultime settimane aveva alimentato le aspettative anche per la sfida al Maradona. Per i sostenitori, la partita con il Napoli rappresentava un appuntamento sentito sia per il valore della gara sia per la volontà di continuare a seguire la formazione biancoceleste in trasferta.

Il divieto interromperebbe questa sequenza di trasferte molto partecipate. Dopo l’esodo di Reggio Emilia, i tifosi residenti nel Lazio rischiano dunque di non poter assistere alla partita di Napoli, neppure acquistando un posto in un settore diverso da quello ospiti.

Shopify Stock Fell After Its Report. Here's the Price I'd Pay for It.

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The e-commerce company's free cash flow climbed 61% last quarter, but the stock fell anyway. Is it cheap enough now?

Shopify (SHOP -8.74%) stock fell nearly 9% on Tuesday after the e-commerce software company posted its third-quarter results. At roughly $144 as I write, shares are about 20% below their Oct. 12 record close of $179.22.

The slide came even though revenue climbed 32% year over year to about $3.7 billion and free cash flow jumped 61% to $816 million.

Before the report, I wrote that I'd rather wait and see where shares settled afterward. The stock was around $157 then, or about 86 times its trailing free cash flow. Now the cash flow has come in slightly over the $800 million I'd penciled in for a strong quarter, and the price is lower. Is it finally time to buy?

Investors seemed to want more than a quarter that landed where management said it would. Shopify's guidance was for third-quarter revenue growth in the low 30s, and 32% delivered that.

But revenue had grown 34% in each of the two previous quarters, beating a low-thirties outlook for the first quarter and a high-twenties forecast for the second.

Gross merchandise volume (GMV, the value of orders placed on Shopify's platform) slowed, too. It rose 29% to $119 billion in the third quarter, after gaining 35% in the first quarter and 32% in the second. Currency explains most of that. In constant currency, GMV grew 30% in all three quarters. Gross profit, though, is where the slowdown shows. Its growth rate dipped to 28% in the third quarter, under the 32% and 31% rates in the first two quarters. And management sees high-twenties growth again in the fourth quarter.

Spending is also set to rise. Management guided fourth-quarter operating expenses to 29% to 30% of revenue. In the fourth quarter of 2025, they were about 29%. Chief financial officer Jeff Hoffmeister said on the call that the forecast reflects more marketing, including some efforts before Black Friday and Cyber Monday. A U.S. marketing test held down spending in the third quarter, and he said part of that spending will move to the fourth.

Taking the middle of each range in the guidance, I get fourth-quarter operating income growth in the high teens. That means profit growth might slow sharply from the 66% Shopify just reported.

I doubt any of these explains Tuesday's fall by itself. But together, they suggest profit growth may ease in the quarters ahead.

Shopify's free cash flow margin (the part of revenue left as cash after capital spending) was 22%, against 18% in the third quarter of 2025. This pushed trailing-12-month free cash flow to around $2.7 billion, up from nearly $2.4 billion through June.

Between the higher cash flow and the lower price, the stock's valuation looks much less stretched than it did last week. At a market cap of around $185 billion, Shopify now trades at about 70 times trailing free cash flow, against about 86 times free cash flow before the report and about 98 times free cash flow at the Oct. 12 record.

Still, not all of that improvement is likely to last. Hoffmeister said only about 1.5 percentage points of the margin gain came from lasting cost discipline. He called the rest more temporary, spread about evenly between the marketing test, the timing of some tax receivables, and an accounting change that shifted merchant cash advances (Shopify's financing for its sellers) out of operating cash flow. Management also expects the fourth-quarter free cash flow margin to roughly match last year's 19%.

Taking out the temporary piece, third-quarter free cash flow would have been closer to $725 million. That comes out to around 72 times trailing free cash flow, just slightly above 70.

Yes, the e-commerce company is still growing quickly and bringing in much more cash than it did a year ago. Shopify even spent $562 million of that cash to buy back its own shares in the third quarter.

But at around 70 times free cash flow, buyers are still paying for years of rapid growth. And they're paying it right as gross profit growth cools and management boosts its marketing.

Does the lower price make Shopify a buy? I think the drop moved the stock closer to a price I'd pay, but not all the way there. I'd start to consider shares somewhere around $115, or about 55 times trailing free cash flow.

That's about 20% under where the stock sits now, so I'm in no rush.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Shopify. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

AMSTERDAM and MOUNTAIN VIEW, Calif., Nov. 4, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- OmnissaÂ®, a leading platform company for securing the agentic endpoint, announced today at Omnissa ONE EMEA the expansion of the Omnissa Sovereign Solution (OSS) to six new markets across Europe and Asia. A fully managed service, OSS is designed to keep customer data, service operations, technical support and the customer contract within the customer's jurisdiction. OSS already supports hundreds of thousands of devices across Austria, Germany, Spain, Sweden and the Czech Republic, including customers in mission-critical and regulated industries.

A safe place for AI to run:As Europe seeks greater control and governance across the AI stack, those same principles of sovereignty must extend beyond where a model is hosted or where data is stored. Trump posted through the U.S. midterm elections, and he wasnt happy

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump has been posting through the U.S. midterm elections, and he doesn't appear happy with the results.

Millions of Americans went to the polls on Tuesday, casting their vote to choose their members of Congress. Democrats decisively took control of both the Senate and House of Representatives in a sweeping midterm victory, one which featured several upsets. South Carolina elected Annie Andrews as its first Democratic senator in 28 years. Texas voted in James Talarico as its first Democratic senator in over 30 years. Even Kansas swung blue, with Adam Hamilton becoming the first Democrat elected to its Senate seat in 94 years.

Still, it's possible that nobody was more upset than Trump. The U.S. president characteristically took to his social media platform Truth Social on election night, expressing his displeasure with the midterm's results as they came in.

Trump's day started off well, the president posting at 12:07 p.m. ET / 9:07 a.m. PT that Guam had been called as a Republican victory. Guam has elected Republican Tony Ada as governor, taking the position from the Democrats for the first time since 2014.

"Guam, which has always been considered strongly Democrat, just had an early call for a REPUBLICAN Victory in the top spot, Governor — Congratulations to Tony Ada!" he wrote. "Also, the Republicans have the Majority in the State Senate. Wow, hopefully that’s an indication for things to come!"

However, Guam's election was separate from the U.S. midterm elections, and had no impact on the makeup of the Senate or House. Once the midterm results began rolling in, the Republicans' situation began looking less rosy.

At 4:11 p.m. ET / 1:11 p.m. PT, Trump shared a screenshot from right-wing YouTuber Benny Johnson in which he claimed that "Republicans are currently showing signs of PRESIDENTIAL-LEVEL midterms turnout already on Election Day." The president also posted that had been watching the pre-election results on Fox News, sharing a clip of commentators praising him. Republican pollster Matt Towery refuted the New York Times' prediction of a "blue surge" in the video, stating that his own polling placed both parties much more evenly matched.

Despite this, Trump began attacking Fox News by 6:36 p.m. ET / 3:36 p.m. PT, shortly after the first polls started to close.

"The FoxNews Poll, always the worst, is so insulting with the questions they ask. Every question is a hit job, and every answer is 'BULLSHIT!'" wrote the U.S. president.

Trump continued to criticise Fox News less than half an hour later at 7:00 p.m. ET / 4:00 p.m. PT, claiming that its ratings are down "because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats." Fox News' viewership is down 27 percent compared to the same time last year, with Fox Corp. CEO Lachlan Murdoch blaming "viewer fatigue" after "some incredible news cycle[s] the last [few] years."

"All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it," Trump wrote. "It’s them, not me — My numbers are better than they have ever been."

Trump's approval rating fell to a record low just prior to the midterm elections, a Reuters/Ipsos poll finding that just 31 percent of Americans approve of his performance in office.

"The new CNN Poll stated that 'TRUMP' is at 99% among Republicans," Trump continued, misinterpreting a 2024 poll in which Trump received 99 percent of the "MAGA GOP" vote specifically when put up against Democrat Kamala Harris.

"Among Republican Presidents, 'TRUMP' is at 53%, compared to Ronald Reagan at 18%, and Abraham Lincoln at 8%!," he wrote, citing a CNN poll asking Republicans which president throughout history they most admire. The same poll found that 64 percent of Democrats most admired former president Barack Obama. Without accounting for party affiliation, 30 percent chose Obama, while 19 percent named Trump.

"No wonder FoxNews Ratings are collapsing, and they’re going to go down a lot further," Trump posted, further blasting Fox Corp. for donating to Democratic candidates. Fox Corp. donates to both Republicans and Democrats. "They’re way down, not because of me, but because they are AGAINST me!"

By the time of Trump's next posts at around 10:37 p.m. ET / 7:37 p.m. PT, the midterm election results were clearly indicating a Democratic sweep.

"I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE! Thank you for your attention to this matter," Trump wrote on Truth Social. "I'm going to become a Political Pundit, both good and bad, on the reasons these Candidates won and lost!"

The president then posted an image of a red cap with the word "veto" embroidered upon it, reminiscent of his "Make America Great Again" hats. Calling in to Fox News, Trump said he was "a little bit surprised" by the midterm election results, and stated that he intended to deal with the Democratic majority Senate and House by blocking its attempts to pass legislation.

"It's the power of veto, it's one of the stronger powers that a president has. And they'll send all the things that are insane and I'll veto them and after a couple of months of vetos they'll come over and they'll say, 'how do we get this stuff done?' and we'll work together," said Trump. "I have the power of veto. The power of veto is impervious."

The U.S. Constitution does allow presidents to refuse to sign legislation, however it is not impassable. Congress can override a president's veto if it has a two-thirds majority in both the House and Senate.

While Trump called in to speak to Fox News, he also blamed the program for the Republicans candidates' poor results. The U.S. president ended midterm election night by claiming on Truth Social that Fox News "bears responsibility for these losses by constantly and consistently putting on the Dumocrat opposition." Many former Republican voters have reported growing disillusionment with the party since Trump took office for his second term, disapproving of how he has handled issues such as the economy and the war in Iran.

Amanda Yeo is an Assistant Editor at Mashable, covering entertainment, culture, tech, science, and social good. Based in Australia, she writes about everything from video games and K-pop to movies and gadgets.

US midterms: Democrats gain control of House and Senate from Republicans

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Democratic Party gained control of the House and Senate, delivering a stinging rebuke to US President Donald Trump and his Republican Party. Follow DW's live updates.

Follow the latest developments from the US midterm elections:

Here you can catch DW's live video coverage of the results and reactions to Democrats flipping both houses of the US Congress, as well as several governors' seats:

Voters chose Rob Sand as Iowa's next governor, defeating first-time Republican candidate Zach Lahn.

The victory is key for Democrats ahead of the 2028 presidential election, because Iowa's early caucuses play a significant role in shaping national campaigns.

Sand, however, had kept the party at arm's length during his race. Instead, he focused on his centrist policies and stressed his humble upbringing.

"We have to have bigger hearts and minds than just our party," Sand told the crowd at a victory event. "Come January, we will be forced to work together."

He promised to work across the aisle to shape Iowa's future.

Former US President Barack Obama said the election results showed Americans wanted leaders who brought people together and offered practical solutions.

With President Donald Trump in office for another two years, Obama urged Democrats to use their gains to pursue change and "prevent further damage."

"Tonight, the American people sent a clear message — they want a politics that brings people together rather than divides them," he wrote on X.

Obama credited strong candidates across the country, including in places once considered unlikely prospects for victory. He also thanked election officials and volunteers from both parties for ensuring a smooth vote.

"Our job now is to roll up our sleeves, make changes wherever we have the power to do so, prevent further damage, and lay the groundwork for the future," he said.

In what was one of the most widely followed and closely watched races of the night, Democratic newcomer Abdul El-Sayed defeated Republican Mike Rogers in Michigan with 52% of the vote to 46%.

The win makes the 42-year-old El-Sayed the first Muslim senator in the history of the United States.

Born and raised in Michigan, El-Sayed was repeatedly targeted by attacks over his heritage and religion, including by President Trump.

Inspired to enter politics after the Flint water crisis that lasted from 2014 to 2019, El-Sayed ran on a platform of making medical care affordable, increasing taxes on billionaires, and building more housing for lower- and middle-income families.

It was one of the most expensive Senate races ever run, with tens of millions poured in from mega-donors on both sides.

"Michigan, we did it!" El-Sayed said as he addressed supporters after his win, to jubilant applause, adding that he was "honored" to have been elected.

He credited his volunteers and staff as well as their message of "Medicare for all and getting money out of politics" for his victory.

Tech mogul Elon Musk appeared to joke on his X account that his "election-interfering space lasers" did not work as Republican candidates he supported began to concede defeat.

At the same time, he commented that he was going to build data centers in space, an apparent reference to how the noise, pollution, and water consumption of data centers had been a hot topic in several congressional races.

Through funding organizations known as PACs, Musk donated $446 million to the races in Maine, Michigan, Texas, and Ohio, amongst others.

Sherrod Brown has defeated Republican Jon Husted in Ohio's special Senate election, securing a victory that had been crucial to Democrats' plan to retake the Senate.

Brown was a longtime Ohio senator until his defeat to Republican Bernie Moreno in 2024. His campaign highlighted the many corruption allegations against Husted, who had been appointed in 2025 to fill the vacant seat left when JD Vance became vice president.

Husted has been accused of being part of a bribery scandal related to his time in Ohio state politics, illegal campaign donations from a billionaire with ties to Jeffrey Epstein.

Despite the tens of millions donated to his campaign and vigorous support from President Trump, Brown won 49% of the vote to Husted's 46%.

"My pledge to all Ohioans, whether you voted for me or you didn't, is that I will always be on your side — always," Brown said in his victory speech. "I do not work for the utility companies. I do not work for data centers. I do not work for Wall Street. I work for you, I work for Ohio."

House Democratic leader Hakeem Jeffries celebrated his party's projected victory, declaring: "America is back."

The result puts him in line to become the first Black Speaker of the House and gives Democrats greater power to challenge President Donald Trump's agenda.

"Today begins the great American comeback," Jeffries told a cheering crowd at the Democrats' watch party, describing the occasion as a patriotic moment rather than a partisan one.

"We're going to work hard to deliver on the dramatic change that you deserve," he said.

Jeffries pledged to prioritize affordable healthcare, fight to reverse Republican cuts to government health insurance, and confront corruption among public officials.

"The American people have spoken and made clear that they've had enough of the extremism, enough of the corruption. They want a Congress that's going to deliver for the American ​people," Jeffries said.

Jeffries would still need to win a vote in the full House when the new Congress convenes in January to become speaker.

Democrats took control of the House of Representatives in the midterm elections, according to Associated Press projections.

This victory, along with wresting control of the US Senate, will likely usher in a new era of opposition to President Donald Trump from the US Congress.

Republicans had held 218 seats to the Democrats' 214 going into the vote.

While seat counts are still being finalized, AP has called Democratic Party wins in 220 seats. A party needs 218 for a majority.

Democrats also flipped the Senate, dealing a heavy blow to Republican governance in the country (see entries below).

Democrat James Talarico has flipped a US Senate seat in the state of Texas.

DW correspondent Misha Komadovsky asked participants of the Democrat watch party in Austin for their reactions.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Claim: "I cannot accept that the result tonight was free or fair. It is simply not possible for somebody with the radical ideology of James Talarico to win an election in Texas fairly," reads an alleged statement from Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton shown on NBC News. The X post of the screengrab was viewed more than 600,000 times.

The facts: Paxton, however, did not say this, and this is not an authentic screenshot of an NBC broadcast. In fact, the opposite is true.

The Republican Senate candidate conceded his race to Talarico.

"Let me start off by congratulating James Talarico. I will commit that I will be praying for him that he will do good for Texas and this nation," Paxton said to a party of campaign supporters.

Talarico won in one of the night's biggest upsets, becoming the first Texan Democrat to win statewide office in three decades.

Susan Collins, the longest-serving female Republican senator in US history, has conceded defeat to Democratic challenger Troy Jackson, local media reported.

Shortly after her concession, the Associated Press also called the race for Jackson.

After two-thirds of the votes were counted, projections had shown Collins at 47.5% to Jackson's 52.4%.

Despite vowing to only serve two terms when she was first elected, Collins has represented the northeastern state in the Senate since 1997.

Maine is often called the "whitest" state due to its lack of diversity as well as having one of the highest rural populations per capita. Although it usually votes Democratic in other races, it has often sent Republican senators to Washington.

Despite its relatively small population, Collins' reputation as a moderate Republican who sometimes voted alongside Democrats often gave her outsized power in Washington, where she was sometimes regarded as a swing voter on Senate legislation.

Democrat James Talarico celebrated with supporters in Austin, saying that "all the money in the world is no match for the people of Texas."

His remarks followed a Texas Senate race in which Elon Musk is estimated to have poured more than $200 million (€180 million) into supporting Republican Ken Paxton.

"We just sent a message to every billionaire mega-donor, every trillionaire mega-donor, every corrupt defender of this broken political system, in this state, in this country, power belongs to the people," Talarico said.

He said the message had been sent to “the richest man in the world, the most powerful man in the world and the entire DC establishment.”

Talarico also invoked the biblical story of David and Goliath to describe the result: "The people of Texas were my slingshot, and tonight we defeated Goliath."

US President Donald Trump acknowledged that the war in Iran and its impact on gasoline prices contributed to Republicans' midterm losses, while expressing surprise at the result.

Projections show Democrats taking control of the Senate and also likely the House of Representatives, giving them greater power to challenge his legislative agenda.

"I think a part of it was Iran, but at the same time people understood the importance of what I've done with Iran," Trump told broadcaster Fox News. He suggested voters had been unwilling to accept the short-term rise in gasoline prices.

"We had tremendous rallies," Trump said. "I'm a little bit surprised." He conceded that Democrats "did a good job."

Trump said he could cooperate with Democrats on several issues, including immigration, but warned that he would veto proposals he opposed.

"They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them," he said. "And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together."

Trump indicated he did not expect to change the members of his Cabinet following the election.

‘Eventually I won’t have a choice’: Car subscriptions for remote start and extra horsepower pile on top of a record $787 average payment

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Asher Davis was vaguely aware that subscription-based car perks had “become a thing,” he said. But when the 33-year-old was thrown into the new-car market unexpectedly, after nine years of blissful ignorance, what he found disturbed him: as far as he could tell, the roster of new cars with no subscriptions attached had dwindled drastically.

“I feel like eventually I won’t have a choice,” he told Fortune.

Davis’s frustration is aimed less at any single subscription—offerings run the gamut from remote start to horsepower upgrades—than at the market’s direction.

Automakers are betting that drivers will get used to paying monthly for features built into cars they’ve already bought. Early evidence suggests consumers will tolerate some of it, but not fees for hardware they’ve already paid for, and their willingness to pay is slipping.

Edmunds doesn’t formally track the spread of subscription-based car features, according to Ivan Drury, the firm’s director of insights. But Drury knows the trend firsthand.

“I’ve got a Subaru that has the location and remote start based upon a subscription fee,” Drury told Fortune. “And my prior vehicle, a 2024 F150, had both of those for free.”

Different manufacturer, different time, he noted, but the contrast is the point. “It’s becoming more frequent and more annoying,” he said.

The appeal for automakers is straightforward. “Number one, it really is based upon the fact that they can,” Drury said. Modern vehicles are connected to the internet, which means features can be switched on and off remotely.

“It’s not like it’s a hard touch point thing,” he said, like swapping leather for cloth. “It’s the fact that there is control over some things that are actually hardwired into the vehicle, but at the same time—flip of a switch.”

The dealership, meanwhile, “hates it,” Drury said. No salesperson wants to sheepishly explain that some of the bells and whistles on a customer’s new toy sit behind a paywall.

“But they’re just the middleman,” Drury said. “It’s shifting where the money’s going, but at the end of the day, who’s bearing the cost? The customer.”

BMW briefly test-drove a subscription for heated seats, charging drivers in markets including the UK, Germany, and South Korea a monthly fee to use seat heaters already installed in their cars. The fee was never offered in the U.S., and the backlash was swift and severe enough that BMW dropped the heated-seat subscription in September 2023.

“Talk about leaving a bad taste in your mouth,” Drury said. “It’s like you bought this vehicle; it’s good to go. You should just be able to hit the button. Get that heated seat. And you paid for a BMW, and you might get yours turned off?”

The episode suggests a red line for consumers: Charging for something long considered part of the car, in a vehicle already sold at a premium, reads differently than charging for a genuinely new capability.

Peter Anderson, 36, a luxury car enthusiast who has been driving high-end cars since age 14 and a half (“thanks to the great state of North Carolina’s legal driving age requirements”), agreed. “Honestly, if the technology is something like heated seats, I don’t want to pay for something that’s already built into the car and I’ve paid for the car to be equipped with those seats,” he told Fortune.

But automakers are banking on his openness to subscriptions in general.

“I’m into [subscriptions] to an extent,” Anderson said. “If there’s an update to the software that’s capable of giving me better performance or a new feature, I’d pay to enable it.”

Anderson cited upgrades to power, handling, and driving dynamics. “Even if it was just a new mode that could be added because the software had evolved,” he said.

Volkswagen, No. 1 on the 2026 Fortune 500 Europe, is testing that appetite. In 2025 it brought its “Power on Demand” subscription for the ID.3 electric hatchback to the UK, letting owners of Pro and Pro S versions pay £16.50 (roughly $22) a month to raise output from 201 to 228 horsepower. The extra 27 horsepower is already built into the car’s hardware.

Online, some Volkswagen customers cried foul (“If you’re OK with this dystopian nonsense, you’re a sucker,” one Redditor said). Volkswagen, meanwhile, told Fortune that customer feedback “has been positive.”

The subscription push arrives as vehicle costs hit records. The average monthly payment on a financed new car reached $787 in the third quarter of 2026, up from $756 a year earlier, according to Edmunds data. The average rate on those loans has held near 7%.

Against payments like that, a $20 or $30 monthly subscription can feel almost negligible, which is exactly what makes it easy to underestimate.

Sue Helper, an economics professor at Case Western Reserve University’s Weatherhead School of Management and a former chief economist at the U.S. Commerce Department, said the monthly sticker price can be a misleading odometer.

“The important thing is to look at the total cost of owning a vehicle, not just the monthly payment,” she told Fortune. “A subscription that costs a few dollars a month may seem insignificant, but multiple fees can add up over several years. Consumers have to weigh those costs against the value they actually get from the features.”

Subscriptions aren’t inherently exploitative, Helper said. They can make sense for services that require ongoing investment, like connectivity or software updates.

Part of the problem is attention, she said. “Consumers have limited time and attention, and they may not fully account for recurring charges when they initially purchase a vehicle.”

She draws the line at charging for hardware already baked into the purchase price. “One difference between a vehicle-related subscription and a magazine subscription is that the former is tied to a very expensive investment,” Helper said. “It wouldn’t be fair to say that the $50,000 vehicle is in fact not usable if a consumer doesn’t pay an ongoing fee.”

She pointed to New York City’s “click to cancel” rule, which Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s administration put into effect Oct. 1, as a policy that can keep tactics that exploit consumer behavior from becoming “standard business practice.”

The risk isn’t only on the consumer side. “A subscription might generate additional revenue in the short term, but if customers feel they’re getting less value for their money, they may be less likely to buy from that company again,” Helper said.

“The competitive question is whether consumers see enough value in a paid feature to keep subscribing—and whether the company’s pricing strategy strengthens or weakens its appeal relative to other brands,” she added.

The market has started to answer. In S&P Global Mobility’s 2025 Connected Car Study, published in July 2025, 68% of respondents said they would pay for connected-car services, down from 86% in 2024.

Automakers are betting the decline is about presentation, not principle: that drivers who balk at a heated-seat fee will still pay for a performance upgrade or a connectivity plan if the value is clear.

Drury worries that the fees will fade into background noise, and that resistance will erode as a new norm sets in. “There’s huge backlash, but at the same time, if even anybody signs up, they make money,” he said.

“Only older people like myself are going to remember that ‘Hey, that used to be free, that used to be something normal that my car always had—and now you guys are charging for it.’”

Sectra Reporting brings imaging, AI, and radiology reporting together in one system at Mount Sinai Health System

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

LINKÃPING, Sweden, Nov. 4, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- International medical imaging IT and cybersecurity company Sectra (STO: SECT B) has signed an agreement with Mount Sinai Health System in New York to deliver Sectra Reporting, the native reporting module in Sectra's enterprise imaging solution, Sectra One Cloud. Mount Sinai's radiologists will go from worklist to signed report in one seamless experience, without switching between systems, helping shorten report turnaround times.

"Radiologists read the image and write the report as one task, and their tools should work the same way. With Sectra Reporting as an integral part of our PACS, reporting evolves together with the imaging platform, and new capabilities, including AI, reach our radiologists without yet another system to integrate," says Dr. David S. Mendelson, Vice Chair of Radiology Information Technology at Mount Sinai Health System.

Mount Sinai Health System is the first health system in the United States to enter a partnership with Sectra for radiology reporting. Sectra Reporting unites imaging, AI, and reporting on a single platform. Mount Sinai is one of the nation's leading integrated academic health systems and one of the largest in the New York metropolitan area, with seven hospitals, more than 400 outpatient practices, over 600 research and clinical laboratories, and the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai. The health system already uses Sectra's enterprise imaging solution for radiology, breast imaging, and enterprise archive. Adding Sectra Reporting to the same platform reduces vendor complexity and simplifies IT management.

The seven-year contract, signed in the second quarter of Sectra's 2026/2027 fiscal year, covers all radiology services, with more than 1.2 million exams per year. Go-live is planned for spring 2027.

"Reporting belongs where the images are. At Sectra, it's part of the same system. Mount Sinai's decision to adopt Sectra Reporting reflects a broader shift toward unified diagnostic workflows, and we look forward to demonstrating what that means for radiologists managing fewer applications and patients who benefit from faster turnaround," says Isaac Zaworski, President of Sectra, Inc.

With Sectra Reporting, radiologists dictate freely while the system automatically structures the report, reviews an AI-drafted impression, and runs quality checks that flag inconsistencies before sign-off.

Sectra's enterprise imaging solution provides a unified strategy for all imaging needs while lowering operational costs. The scalable and modular solution, with a VNA at its core, allows healthcare providers to grow from ology to ology and from enterprise to enterprise.
