QuantStreet Capital November 2026 Update

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With the one constant being that gold fell again, this time by 6.6%, Octoberâs price action is a mirror image of what happened in markets in September. In September, tech and momentum led the charge higher, with anything value- or rate-related leading the selloff. October was the exact opposite. Utilities, financials, value, and all things rate-related had sharp rallies, while all things tech had a horrendous month, with Nasdaq down 5.3% and EM ex-China (i.e., largely Korea and Taiwan and their semiconductor sectors) down close to 10%. A broader index of international stocks did not fare very well either, seeing a drop of 3% on the month. Fixed income markets, after being positively trounced in September, had a bit of a bounce with high-yield bonds up 0.8%, investment grade and municipal bonds up 2.2% and 3.5% respectively, and medium-duration Treasuries up 2.3%.

At QuantStreet, our portfolios had some bad calls and some good ones. Our small gold exposure hurt the portfolio (for the second month in a row), our international exposure did not fare well either, while our overweight in value and low-vol stocks helped (after having done poorly the month before). Overall, the portfolios performed in-line with benchmarks, with some risk levels doing worse and some doing better than their benchmarks. You can see our absolute performance numbers here. If you would like to see relative performance numbers, please email us at hello@quantstreetcapital. com.

It is telling that many of the journalists that I regularly read and respect wrote very negative pieces about the AI trade and markets more broadly in October.

Charlie Gasparino of the NY Post recently wrote about the parallels between the dot-com bubble and the AI buildout, predicting that âthe correction is coming to AI.â A mid-October article from James Mackintosh of the WSJ warned that AI companies would have a hard time avoiding the overinvestment trap of prior technological revolutions. Mackintosh argues that concerns about this overbuild are leading to lower price-to-forward-earnings multiples (more on this below) as âinvestors are paying less per dollar of the earnings coming up in the next year, because they know it wonât last.â

Indeed, the pace of investing by the hyperscalers (Amazon (AMZN), Google (GOOGL), Meta (META), Microsoft (MSFT), Oracle (ORCL)) has been remarkable. Collectively, theyâve spent $690bn in capital expenditures (CAPEX) over the last twelve months ((LTM)), with that amount representing a $334bn increase from the LTM spend from a year ago. (Interestingly, the LTM net income of these companies looks very similar to, though a bit lower than, their LTM capex. So, at least, the additional investment is of the same order of magnitude as current profits.)

A more recent WSJ article from James Mackintosh talks about the âtangled web of AI betsâ as one reason to be wary of the AI boom. Another WSJ piece, from Gunjan Banerji, documents how investors are looking to buy insurance against an AI selloff using either equity derivatives or credit default swaps. Boston Universityâs Larry Kotlikoff, in a recent Substack piece, warns about an impending stock market collapse due to AI overinvestment.

In short, there is no lack of negative narratives about how anything AI-related might go belly up in a big way. While there is of course endogeneityâthese articles were all written after the start of Octoberâs market selloffâthe causality likely runs in the other direction too (see my JFQA paper on this topic). Such narratives reflect persistent investor fears and depress valuations.

One data point that supports a cheerier outlook is that a lot of smart people are warning about the dangers of AI overinvestment. Investors and firms are paying attention. If the AI trade does indeed blow up one day, it wonât be because no one saw it coming. And the fact that so many people see it coming might, perversely, mean that the blow up never arrives. Another cheerful data point comes from US macro data. As of 2Q 2026, corporate after-tax profits (unadjusted) grew 26.6% in nominal terms year-over-year, while the latest 3Q 2026 GDP data showed 5.9% year-over-year nominal growth. These are heady numbers indeed. Of course, the naysayers are concerned that such growth rates are not sustainable. But, if they turn out to be, the gloomy market narratives are likely misplaced.

The confluence of the above narrativesâwhether cause or consequenceâwas associated with a tough October for US, tech and international stocks. Despite this one month, all three markets remain well up on the year: S&P 500 is up 11.75% year-to-date, the Nasdaq-100 is up 12.98%, and developed international stocks (MSCI World ex-US) are up 10.55%.

As already mentioned, price-to-forward-earnings multiples for these (and other) markets are well off their highs at this point. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq-100 are both trading at a two-year forward multiple of under 16. In fact, the Nasdaq-100 multiple is slightly lower than the S&P 500 one at this point, which is quite unusual. These multiples make the markets look downright reasonably valued, and should give pause to those using elevated CAPE ratios (price to average earnings over the last ten years) to argue that markets are overvalued: earnings per share (EPS) have grown meaningfully over the last ten years and the cyclically-adjusted earnings part of CAPE unfairly understates the current level of corporate profitability.

Of course whatâs really bothering people is that current EPS levels are not sustainable. The next chart shows EPS growth for US, tech, and non-US international stocks over the last five years (all series are normalized to start at 100). S&P 500 EPS has grown at a 12.4% CAGR over the last five years. The concerns about AI overinvestment hinge on the idea that if the investments donât pan out, corporate profitability will fall, and (as James Mackintosh wrote) these EPS levels will not be sustainable.

Itâs hard, or impossible, to know how future corporate earnings will evolve, but the currently insatiable demand for compute combined with robust GDP growth in the US suggest that the good times might continue for some time yet.

Our thesis has been that the AI trade will, at some point, have to transition from the AI creators to the AI users. We wrote that September price action was worrying because the rest of the corporate sector was getting crushed while the AI creators (tech, semis) were doing great. That seemed to us a non-sustainable dynamic. Octoberâs price action was the exact opposite: the companies that will ultimately benefit from AI rallied, while the AI providers were the ones that sold off. (This Vanguard piece lays out a similar rest-of-corporate-America will benefit from AI thesis.) We also continue to hold a sizable value exposure, as well as a small allocation to low volatility stocks, a sector which looks a bit like the equal-weighted S&P 500, with large weights in technology, healthcare, and financials.

In addition to these lower-volatility names (value is also less volatile than the broad market), we introduce a bit of a barbell exposure into the portfolios, with allocations to high beta sectors, like EM ex-China and tech stocks. We like the EM exposure for two reasons: first, it provides exposure to world-class semiconductors companies; second, it benefits from structural tailwinds, e.g., low leverage, improving corporate governance and focus on profitability, as laid out in a recent JP Morgan report. We like tech because it does really well on days when the broader market rallies, thus providing needed beta exposure to the portfolios. And the valuation story (see discussion above) strikes us as a compelling catalyst. The downside is that, should the AI naysayers prove correct, both of these positions will be very exposed.

As an aside, the reason we like EM exposure of the ex-China variety is well captured by this next chart from the JP Morgan report. While Chinaâs GDP growth has been extraordinary, the pass-through of that growth to corporate earnings has been meager. The analogous chart for the rest of the world shows earnings growth handily beating GDP growth. As long as the GDP-earnings pass-through isnât âworkingâ in China, it is hard to see how Chinese stocks do well relative to their global peers.

On the fixed income side, we maintain part of our exposure in the 5- to 7-year Treasury space, while keeping the core 2-year Treasury position that weâve carried for a long time now. This leaves us still relatively short duration compared to our benchmarks, an exposure which we like across model, trend, and fundamental (i.e., strong growth) dimensions.

Investing in financial markets involves risks. Investors may lose money. Our investment strategies cannot ensure a profit or protect against loss. There are many factors that affect future risks and returns that we are unable to anticipate. Past performance does not guarantee future results, which may be materially worse.

Forecasting models may do a poor job of describing actual market outcomes and any estimates of expected returns, including efforts to achieve a particular return for a given level of risk, are inherently uncertain and may not be realized. Strategies that are intended to replicate the performance of target-date funds may not achieve this goal and may produce materially worse results.

Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A live walkthrough of the Agents CLI eval loop, from a first run to regression tracking, model-backend experiments, and automated alerting

Building an agent that works on a demo is a different problem from building an agent that stays working across model upgrades, tool changes, and new conversation shapes. The first takes a weekend; the second takes a loop. A team writes test cases, runs them, grades them, reads what failed, changes the agent, and runs the cases again. If the score goes up, the change was good. If it goes down, revert. Over weeks of this, the agent inches toward the ceiling of what the model, tools, and prompt can do together. The quality-engineering name for this is hill-climbing.

The Agents CLI eval namespace was built around this loop. The commands are small and framework-agnostic: eval generate runs the agent against a dataset of cases, eval grade scores the generated traces, eval compare diffs two run results side by side, eval analyze clusters failures into themes, and eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt.

Below: baseline → regression → model swap → scheduling and alerting.

The scaffold used throughout is the LangChain template that ships with Agents CLI v1.8.0. The setup is a single command:

The scaffold ships with an eval dataset at tests/eval/datasets/basic-dataset.json, a config at tests/eval/eval_config.yaml, and a custom LLM-as-judge metric at tests/eval/response_quality.py. The config declares the metric to run:

The metric grades each response on a 1-to-5 scale for accuracy, relevance, and clarity, using gemini-3.8-flash as the judge. The default dataset has a single trivial case, which is not enough for a hill-climb. The first thing we do is expand it to four cases that probe different behaviors:

Four cases: a greeting, a weather question the mock tool handles with the hardcoded foggy response, a weather question for a city the tool defaults on, and an off-topic question that the agent has to decide how to handle. This is a small dataset by design; the hill-climbing loop pays off when the dataset is small enough to run on every change.

With the dataset in place, we run eval generate to drive the agent against each case, then eval grade to score the resulting traces:

Mean 4.0 out of 5 across four cases, with a standard deviation of 1.41. That stdev tells us immediately that the cases are not scoring uniformly, which is a healthier starting point than a flat 5.0 across the board. A flat 5.0 either means the agent is perfect (unlikely) or the dataset is too easy to show variation. 4.0 with real spread is a hill to climb.

To demonstrate the loop, we introduce a realistic regression. A developer working across several agent projects accidentally pastes the wrong system prompt into our weather agent: a chef persona meant for a different codebase. In app/agent.py:

The change compiles, the agent still runs, and nothing in the pre-eval pipeline (lint, unit tests, type checks) catches it. Re-running the same eval generate plus eval grade sequence produces:

Mean 2.75, down from 4.0. The judge agrees with our intuition that a chef persona on a weather agent is a worse product than a general-purpose helpful assistant. The stdev also tightened from 1.41 to 0.96, consistent with the chef prompt pushing most cases toward the same mediocre range.

A 1.25-point drop on a 1-to-5 scale is substantial, and the kind of regression that can slip through code review when the diff looks small ("one string literal changed"). The loop surfaced the change at all, with a specific number attached to it, before the agent reached production.

Mean scores are a start; the per-case diff is where the hill-climb happens. eval compare reads two result files and produces a structured JSON diff:

The output lists which keys changed and which stayed the same:

The differences object has the full per-case breakdown with the baseline and candidate values side by side. For a hill-climbing workflow, we typically pipe the output through jq or a small Python script to extract the signal; the raw JSON is the complete evidence.

Walking the two result files and lining up the scores per case shows where the regression landed:

Three cases dropped, one held. The most important of the three is sf_weather: the chef persona pulled the model's attention away from the registered get_weather tool, so the agent stopped calling it and started making up weather values. Unit tests can't catch this: the code runs, the tool is still registered, but the model's behavior around it has shifted. Only an end-to-end eval that scores the response in context surfaces this.

The mean drop by itself (−1.25) is a clear signal, but the per-case breakdown is what tells the developer where to look. A one-line system-prompt change propagated through three different cases in three different ways: greeting lost a point on tone, sf_weather lost two on tool usage, off_topic lost two on scope creep.

A common hill-climbing question is whether a cheaper or faster model would do the job acceptably. For the LangChain template, the model is defined in app/agent.py:

We swap gemini-3.8-flash for gemini-2.5-flash (restoring the helpful system prompt first), re-run eval generate plus eval grade, and compare the two result files. The mean drops from 4.00 to 2.75, the same magnitude as the chef-prompt regression, but the per-case breakdown tells a completely different story:

The cheaper model matched 3.8-flash exactly on the greeting and rome_weather cases and lost a point on sf_weather (slightly less polished phrasing). The whole visible drop traces to off_topic, where the cheaper model refused with a hedge about the event "not having happened yet" (a training-cutoff artifact), and the judge marked it harshly.

The lesson is that the summary number is misleading. Look only at the mean and you'd call gemini-2.5-flash as bad as the chef prompt. Look at the per-case breakdown and you'd call it a reasonable swap for most of the agent's workload, with one case (handling out-of-scope questions about recent events) exposing a real limitation. If the production distribution of user questions matches the first three cases more than the fourth, the swap is worth considering. If off_topic-style questions are common, it is not.

Two caveats on this experiment. First, model swaps interact with the prompt: a prompt tuned for a stronger model often leaks capability when ported to a weaker one, so the right comparison is "weaker model with a prompt tuned for it" versus "stronger model with its own tuned prompt." Second, the Vertex AI Eval Service calls a judge model to grade the traces, and the judge is held constant across the comparison. Changing the judge is a different experiment and should be done separately.

Agents CLI does not ship a scheduler, but the eval commands are designed to drop into standard infrastructure. The pattern we can use here is a cron job or Cloud Scheduler trigger that runs eval generate plus eval grade on a cadence and writes the results to a persistent location:

A nightly run against a stable regression dataset catches drift from model updates, data changes in the agent's tools, and dependencies that shift under the agent's feet. For a production agent, the scheduled run also captures the model vendor's quiet upgrades, which can move the agent's behavior without any change on our side. The dataset for the scheduled run is typically larger than the one used in the inner loop (dozens to hundreds of cases rather than a few), since it only runs once a day.

The scheduled run produces a result file every day, but a file nobody looks at is not an alert. The pattern here can be a wrapper script that compares each new run's mean against a baseline (the previous run, or a committed known-good) and exits non-zero when the drop exceeds a threshold. The non-zero exit is what triggers the alert from the surrounding scheduler.

Running it against our regression demo (baseline 4.0, candidate 2.75 from the chef-prompt run):

And against an identical pair (self-compare):

The non-zero exit code is what a cron job, Cloud Scheduler, or GitHub Actions step picks up to send the alert.

For ADK projects, eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt using the GEPA framework (not currently supported for the LangChain extension). The command takes a dataset and a target metric, proposes prompt variants, grades each one, keeps the variants that improve the metric, and iterates. The output is a set of candidate prompts ranked by their eval score, which a human then reviews and promotes to the agent definition.

Two things to understand about eval optimize. First, it is scoped to prompt optimization, not to arbitrary agent changes: the agent's tools, structure, and model stay fixed. Second, GEPA sometimes finds genuine improvements and sometimes finds prompt variants that score higher on the specific metric by overfitting to its wording. The reviewer is the control. For projects where the loop applies, the discipline is to run eval optimize on the regression dataset (where improvements are measurable) and to review the candidate prompts carefully before promotion.

A few patterns and anti-patterns from running this loop ourselves and watching teams do the same.

Lead with a small, representative dataset, not a comprehensive one. The temptation when starting an eval is to try to cover every edge case the agent will ever see. The result is a dataset that takes an hour to run and that nobody runs on a change. Four cases that run in under a minute, as above, exercise the main behaviors and get run on every change. A regression dataset of three hundred cases that runs for an hour becomes a release gate at best and shelfware at worst.

Snapshot result files, not scores. The score is a summary; the result file is the evidence. When a regression surfaces three weeks after a prompt change, having the full result JSON from before and after the change (not just the mean score) is what lets eval compare show the specific case that moved. The per-case breakdown in Step 4 above would not have been possible from scores alone.

Separate the dataset from its expectations. The dataset is the inputs. The eval config is the expectations. They evolve on different cadences: the dataset grows as new real-world cases surface in production, and the config evolves as the team's definition of "good" matures. Keeping them in separate files, both committed to version control, makes it clear which half changed when a score moves.

Treat summary metrics with suspicion. The model-swap experiment in Step 5 is the clearest example. The mean dropped by half a point, but the per-case breakdown showed the drop was concentrated in one case while the others held or improved. A team that only looked at the mean would have made the wrong call.

More evals do not automatically lead to more fixes. A recent controlled study we ran found that coding agents using agents-cli run four times as many evals as coding agents without it, but change the agent after an eval about as often as the baseline. The behavior gain comes from what the skills put into the build at the start, not from the inner iteration. Evals are necessary for quality, but the quality comes from acting on what the evals show.

Scaffold an Agents CLI project and run the first eval loop in a handful of commands:

The full eval namespace is documented at google.github.io/agents-cli/cli. The source code, including the first-party eval primitives, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

LiteRT in Google Play services is Android's on-device inference runtime for custom models, handling more than a trillion inferences a day across billions of devices. Today, it gets its biggest upgrade yet: GPU inference that runs about 1.4x faster than the legacy GPU delegate, powered by ML Drift and available through the new CompiledModel API, LiteRT’s modern successor to the legacy Interpreter API.

Until now, taking advantage of this faster GPU stack required bundling LiteRT in your app. In our testing, apps that switched to LiteRT in Google Play services are about 8 MB smaller, making them faster to download and lighter on users' devices, because the runtime, including the GPU accelerator, ships once with Play services rather than inside every app. More than 100K Android apps bundle their own copy of LiteRT or TensorFlow Lite today, so the savings add up quickly as apps switch.

If your Android app runs custom ML models on CPU or GPU, we encourage you to switch to LiteRT in Google Play services. You can see it in action in the Google AI Edge Gallery app, where the Scrapbook feature's image segmentation runs on LiteRT in Google Play services.

Developers that adopt LiteRT in Google Play Services enjoy immediate benefits:

LiteRT is the evolution of TensorFlow Lite. It keeps the same .tflite model format you rely on and adds the CompiledModel API, which makes acceleration across fragmented hardware reliable:

Whether you're starting fresh or switching from bundled LiteRT, using LiteRT in Google Play services takes a few lines of code:

If you're already using the legacy Interpreter API in Google Play services, your Interpreter code keeps working on CPU when you switch to play-services-litert. To run on the GPU, move to the CompiledModel API.

Let your coding agent do the work. In Agent Mode, the new LiteRT skill for Android Studio automates the move to LiteRT in Google Play services and the CompiledModel API. Ask it to “migrate my TFLite code to CompiledModel,” or type @litert-runtime, and it switches your app to play-services-litert, rewrites your Interpreter calls, and sets up GPU acceleration with CPU fallback. It can also scaffold a new LiteRT app. Treat it as a strong first pass: review the diff, build, and compare outputs on a real device. Get the skill on GitHub.

Upgrading from legacy APIs? The legacy GPU delegate and custom delegate APIs are deprecated in favor of CompiledModel GPU acceleration and the Compiler Plugin and Dispatch APIs, and NNAPI will be removed starting in 2027. Older SDKs now receive critical security fixes only. Apps you've already shipped keep working, but plan your move to the CompiledModel API now: code that uses deprecated APIs won't compile against the latest SDK, and staying on an older SDK can cause conflicts as your other dependencies move forward. See the release notes for details.

Looking ahead, LiteRT in Google Play services is our foundation for running your own task-specific models on Android, with CPU and GPU acceleration.

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agent-User Interactions are now evolving from text exchanges to rich UIs. Until recently, most conversations looked like this:

User: "Find me a holiday package to Bali in March." Agent: (six packages in prose, each with dates, hotel, flights, price) User: "The five-night one with breakfast." Agent: "Here's the link. You can book it on the operator's site."

Agents could plan the trip, but everything came back as walls of text. They had no standard way to render a selectable list of packages, a map of the destinations, or a date picker to carry you through the rest of the booking, inside the conversation itself.

Generative UI is the layer that lets agents build interfaces at runtime, shaped by what each request needs - giving users a rich, interactive experience. Implementing that takes two pieces: a way to describe the UI and an agentic stack that connects your agent to your application and renders that UI.

Generative UI covers a spectrum of approaches, from using predefined higher order components, to enabling your AI agent to have the flexibility to decide how to build the UI.

At the controlled end, you build each component ahead of time, and the agent picks which one to render and with what data. It's brand-safe but it does not allow for deep customization and personalization based on the user’s intent. At the open end, the agent returns a raw UI program written in HTML, Javascript and/or Dart that renders inside a sandboxed iframe. That covers anything you did not plan for, at the cost of a surface that drifts from your design system and executes code a model wrote.

A2UI works through the entire spectrum. It participates in controlled generation with decision models like Typesafe’s Jev and also open-ended code generation that may output A2UI JSON messages. The declarative mode, where agents assemble pre-declared components and functions, is the most popular. A2UI is the framework-agnostic standard for declaring UI intent, so local or remote agents can generate your UI from your existing component catalog using a common language.

Frontend developers can now ship the building blocks once, and the agent composes them for each request.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The Basic Catalog ships 18 general-purpose primitives across layout, display, input, and containers. The agent can compose entirely different interfaces from the same 18 primitives, depending on what the request needs.

The Basic Catalog is deliberately sparse, so most production apps define their own custom catalog. A custom component can be anything your frontend already renders, including a live map the agent fills with coordinates while your renderer draws the route.

CopilotKit is built on AG-UI (Agent-User Interaction Protocol), which supports A2UI payload and provides Agent SDKs across many agentic backends.

Your agent emits A2UI operations. AG-UI carries them to the client as typed events. When the user takes any action, the values are sent back along the same stream.

A2UI works with any AG-UI compatible agent. Frameworks like Google ADK, LangChain, Mastra and Strands are all AG-UI compatible, so the setup is identical whichever one your agent runs on.

Setup comes down to two things: connecting your agent to CopilotKit, and defining which components the agent is allowed to use.

Using Google ADK as the example, the ag_ui_adk package wraps any ADK agent and serves it as an AG-UI endpoint on FastAPI:

On the app side, you register that endpoint with the CopilotKit runtime at /api/copilotkit. It's a few lines of standard setup, covered in the Google ADK quickstart, and it needs nothing A2UI-specific.

A2UI itself turns on from the client, where your components already are. Define what the agent is allowed to draw, map each definition to a renderer, and pass the catalog to the CopilotKit provider.

The description goes into the agent's prompt, so the model knows when to reach for FlightCard instead of a generic card. The Zod schema validates the props it sends back.

The A2UI configuration can be set up ahead of time on the server, or it can be created dynamically, shipped from the client to the server with the request. The ADK code above used dynamic configuration which is why it used generate_a2ui without defining it. The docs cover both approaches.

That’s the CopilotKit path. You can also consume the event stream directly with @ag-ui/client and hand the A2UI operations to any compliant renderer: @a2ui/react, or the Flutter, Lit, or Angular equivalent.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Most agent-generated UI is one-shot. The agent draws something, you read it, and it goes stale the moment anything changes.

A2UI surfaces are bi-directional and the components are reactive, which makes multi-step flows possible. Any action the user takes on the interface (picking dates, choosing a flight, entering passenger details) travels back along the same stream, and the agent re-emits the surface from there using those values.

Here is a high-level architecture example of an end-to-end booking flow, with an AG-UI agent on Cloud Run streaming A2UI surfaces to a CopilotKit frontend.

Two things make the round trip work: inputs bound to a data path, and a button carrying an action that names which paths to send back.

Validation runs on the client, so a bad email shows an error inline without a model call. Nothing reaches the agent until the button fires.

Then the client sends the bound values, and the agent re-emits against the same surfaceId. That is how the whole flow lives on one surface: flight search, results, passenger details, and confirmation.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Every AG-UI integration inherets these benefits, so you don't have to build them yourself:

Create a complete A2UI + CopilotKit application. This creates a starter A2UI application with Copilotkit Agent integration.

The CopilotKit Dojo has runnable A2UI demos: fixed server-side schema, dynamic schema against a custom catalog, and an error-recovery example where an invalid first render heals into a valid one.

Explore how to build Generative UI Agents with CopilotKit, or dive into A2UI documentation to learn how to integrate A2UI with any framework of your choice.

Rig of Troy: A Suspicious Recovery Script Hidden Inside Roblox Toolbox Models

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An investigation into suspicious R6 models, hidden GUIs, remote code loading, and a possible social engineering attack.

R6 models and animations in the Roblox Toolbox.

Everything seemed normal until I inspected one of the models more closely.

Inside the character's Right Arm, I found something unexpected: a script called Runtime.lua, accompanied by a complex hierarchy of objects that seemed completely unrelated to the model's intended purpose.

At first, I thought it was just an unusually complicated animation system.

Further investigation revealed suspicious scripts, hidden interface components, and code designed to convince developers to execute an external command.

I decided to call this investigation Operation Rig of Troy.

After finding the first suspicious model, I started examining other assets from the Toolbox.

Surprisingly, I encountered very similar structures in multiple models, sometimes published by different creators.

One recurring hierarchy looked like this:

The most interesting detail was the location of the GUI.

In the models I examined, suspicious interface components repeatedly appeared under MovementElevateAction.

Why would an R6 animation need an entire interface system hidden inside a character's arm?

There might be legitimate explanations for unusual object hierarchies, but the repeated structure justified further investigation.

I examined the source code without intentionally executing the suspicious recovery command.

This code only proceeds while running in Roblox Studio.

The script then locates a ScreenGui named InputBinding inside the MovementElevateAction hierarchy.

After finding the interface, it clones the stored objects into the player's PlayerGui.

During installation, it also renames several components:

The script also contains a fingerprinting mechanism based on object attributes and names, along with checks that can stop the installation under certain conditions.

This confirmed that the script was installing an interface, not simply controlling an animation.

However, this script alone did not prove that a malicious remote module was being executed.

The next important discovery was a client-side script named WindowDriver.lua.

This script was responsible for building and displaying the interface.

One particularly interesting section waited until the script had detected sufficient user activity before presenting the notification.

This made the interface appear during an active Studio testing session.

The script also assembled its text from attributes stored in the GUI rather than keeping all messages directly in the source code.

Several attributes were named Row22, Row23, and so on.

These attributes contained fragments of a Lua command.

The suspicious command appeared designed to retrieve a module identifier from an external endpoint and load the corresponding module.

Even more interestingly, the interface used two sets of attributes:

Rows 22–27: The actual text placed inside the selectable text field.

Rows 80–85: The text rendered in the syntax-highlighted preview.

The script made the underlying text transparent and displayed a separately rendered version above it.

This design could potentially display different text from what the user copies. However, I did not establish that the two versions were different in the examined sample.

The confirmed concern was already significant: a model was presenting instructions that encouraged the developer to execute externally sourced code in Roblox Studio.

This is consistent with a social engineering technique, rather than an ordinary animation dependency.

This is where the distinction between evidence and assumptions matters.

However, I did not identify or reverse engineer the final remotely loaded module.

Therefore, I cannot conclusively state what the final payload would do.

It might have been intended to install a backdoor, but confirming that requires additional evidence.

I also cannot establish who originally created the scripts or whether every model containing the same structure was distributed by the same actor.

The investigation illustrates why Roblox Toolbox assets should not automatically be trusted.

A free model may contain much more than its visible parts.

Importantly, do not execute unknown malware simply to see what happens. Static inspection can already reveal significant information.

Instead, I found a suspicious runtime system, unusual GUI structures, a hidden window driver, and a potential social engineering mechanism involving remote code execution.

I did not prove the existence of a functioning backdoor payload, but I documented a concerning method of persuading developers to execute untrusted code.

For me, this investigation was also an introduction to practical cybersecurity research: examining source code, comparing artifacts, identifying suspicious behavior, and separating facts from hypotheses.

And everything started with a character's arm.

Disclaimer: This article documents static code analysis and observations from Roblox Studio. It does not claim to identify the original developer of the suspicious code or prove the functionality of an unexamined remote payload.

We Dropped Harbor for Large Images. A Traefik Default Was the Cause.

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 4 • Originally published at amalzacharia.com • AI-assisted

I stopped pushing large images to Harbor because the pushes kept failing. The cause was a default in Traefik, which I had adopted only because ingress-nginx was retired.

It took me a while to see that, and the reason is the useful part. Every metric I had said the system was healthy.

The cluster is new and runs on bare metal. Only HTTP and HTTPS need to be reachable from outside. Everything else is consumed inside the private network or the cluster, where eBGP handles routing fine.

For the public side I used Cloudflare Tunnel. A tunnel needs no public IP on the cluster. Cilium's L2 announcements were still beta and send all inbound traffic for an address to a single node, so I didn't want to build the edge on them. The tunnel also removed two chores. I didn't have to set up authenticated origin pulls, and I didn't have to maintain a firewall that blocks every IP outside Cloudflare's ranges, because the origin only makes outbound connections.

The request path is Cloudflare's edge, the tunnel, Traefik, then the service. Traefik was my ingress because ingress-nginx, the previous default, was retired in March 2026. I knew nginx's timeouts well. I knew almost nothing about Traefik's.

My images lived on GHCR. GitHub's docs say fine-grained tokens don't cover Packages, and I found no native way to issue a pull credential limited to part of an account's images. Harbor has project-level RBAC and robot accounts that stay inside one project, so I ran Harbor in the cluster.

CI pushed built images to it. Small images went through. Large ones timed out, and not every time. I tried to work around it from the client side, and the workarounds were flaky. In the end I stopped sending anything over 100 MB through Harbor.

I put the failures down to something in front of Harbor, most likely Cloudflare. Prometheus had nothing to contradict that. It had nothing to confirm it either.

A separate project needed object storage. It builds kernel and root filesystem images for microVMs, each a few gigabytes. They lived in a hosting provider's object store, and the provider had no region near the nodes that run the microVMs. Whenever the artifact cache expired or a new artifact shipped, every node pulled it from another region over a congested public route.

So I moved storage into the cluster: an S3-compatible store behind the same tunnel and the same Traefik. The first publish was a huge multipart upload, and I split it into 100 MB parts.

Splitting the upload into 100 MB parts didn't stop the failures. The cloudflared pod's CPU and memory sat flat. Nothing was overloaded, so something configured was cutting the connections.

The public internet doesn't guarantee throughput, which matches the pattern. A part that hit a slow stretch took longer than the others, and only those parts failed.

The cluster was new, so observability was already in place. Prometheus and Loki had told me nothing useful. Tempo was the missing piece. I pointed Traefik's tracing at it, turned on Cloudflare's trace export to the same place, and retried the upload.

Cloudflare saw the origin answer 504 after about 60.2 seconds. Traefik's own span ended at exactly 60.0 seconds. A network fault doesn't end at a round number.

Traefik's entrypoint readTimeout defaults to 60 seconds, and the docs say it covers reading the entire request, including the body. A multipart part is one request. Any part whose body took longer than a minute to arrive was cut with a 504, whatever the client was configured to tolerate.

That explained everything I had seen. The failures were intermittent because the speed of the public route varied, and client-side workarounds did nothing because the limit lived on the server. Prometheus showed nothing because a timeout adds no load.

nginx behaves differently. Its client_body_timeout limits the gap between two successive reads of the body, so a slow but steady upload survives it. Traefik's limit covers the whole request. I had no reason to know that difference until I migrated.

I retried the huge upload and it went through cleanly. Then I retried Harbor, and the large pushes that used to fail succeeded.

An intermittent failure that ends at a round number is a timeout until something proves otherwise. Metrics measure load, and a timeout adds none, so the dashboards can stay green the whole time.

When a deprecation forces you onto a new component, read its timeout defaults before you send your largest request through it. Put traces in before you need them. The hop I couldn't see was the one at fault.

I dropped Harbor for large images on a wrong explanation, and I only found the right one because a different project forced me to look.

nexus studioQA 14

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A red test can mean three very different things: the application broke, the network blinked, or the site showed a bot challenge. Most tools report all three as "failed", so teams learn to rerun reds instead of reading them.

Nexus Studio, by Verdict System LLC, gives every run one of 14 verdicts. Here is one run of seven tests and what each one said:

SECURE: passed on its own. FLAKY: failed, then a retry passed it. Nothing was fixed. BREACHED: the application broke. A real finding. TIMEOUT: never answered in time. CRASHED: the browser died. BLOCKED: a 403, a 429 or a bot challenge. UNREACHABLE: the host could not be reached. Only BREACHED is a bug report. The other failures say the environment or the harness is at fault, and a retry never turns a defect into a green run.

Every failure leaves a folder of evidence: a forensic report you can read without the Studio, a screenshot, console and network data, a video for a real defect, and an optional SHA-256 chain of custody. An Executive Report turns the history into a go, caution or no-go call.

How the verdicts are decided: https://nexusstudioqa.com/docs/verdicts

By the Nexus Studio team, Verdict System LLC.

The pause before the action: how Sakupa's confirmation step works

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A quick disclosure first: I am not Sakupa's developer. I came across this MCP server while looking into how AI agents publish websites, and this post only organizes facts from its official site and official manual. This is not a hands-on review.

Sakupa is an MCP server that lets an AI agent publish and operate a static website without account registration. This time I want to focus on one design choice: before anything is published or changed, the agent has to stop and show you what it is about to do.

The pattern, as described in the official manual, is simple: analyze first, preview the change, confirm, and only then execute. The agent does not go straight from your request to a live change. It prepares the change, shows it, and waits for confirmation.

This matters more than it sounds. When you ask an AI to change a website, the most common failure is not a dramatic one. It is a small one: the wrong date, the wrong page, a wording change you did not mean. A preview step catches those before they go live, when fixing them is still cheap.

It is tempting to read a confirmation step as friction. But in Sakupa's case, the pause does three quiet jobs:

The same idea shows up elsewhere in Sakupa. Payments go through a Stripe-hosted page rather than through the server itself. Credentials stay on the user's own machine. The design keeps the sensitive parts in places where they can be checked.

A confirmation step does not decide whether the change is a good idea. It only shows you what the change is. The content — the hours, the menu, the announcement — is still yours to decide. And Sakupa only handles static sites, so anything needing a database or login functionality is out of scope. A contact form is available as the first app in its catalog, with its own page-side contract and a verification code for the notification address.

When an AI agent can act on your behalf, the interesting question is not how fast it acts. It is where it stops and asks. Sakupa's answer is: before every publish and every change, with a preview you can actually read. If you are curious, the 30-day free preview is a reasonable way to try that flow once, on a small change, before committing to a plan that starts at ¥200 per month per site.

How Sybil Detection Algorithms Flag Automated Farmers in 2026 (And the Math Behind Mouse Micro-Jitter)

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 4 • Originally published at multitasksautoclick.nimbokit.com

For years, multi-accounting in Web3 and MMOs was simple: buy residential proxies, configure 20 profiles in an anti-detect browser like AdsPower, GoLogin, or MoreLogin, and run a basic clicker script or AutoHotkey loop.

In 2026, leading Layer 1/Layer 2 ecosystems alongside enterprise fraud engines (Cloudflare Turnstile, Arkose Labs) have deployed Behavioral Biometric Telemetry.

Even if your IP is a pristine static residential proxy and your Canvas/WebGL fingerprint is randomized, heuristic algorithms analyze the physical telemetry of your interactions. When 50 different browser profiles perform daily check-ins with mechanically identical click patterns, those profiles are clustered and flagged as a Sybil Cluster in seconds.

Standard tools like OP Auto Clicker, GS Auto Clicker, or unthrottled Python pyautogui scripts trigger bot detection via three fundamental mechanical signatures:

Humans cannot click the exact same pixel (X₀, Y₀) repeatedly. Physiological tremors in the fingers and wrist cause subtle variations of ±1 to ±3 pixels.

Basic clickers pass the exact same integer coordinates on every event call. When an analytics heatmap reveals 100 clicks on (450, 720) with 0 variance, you are instantly categorized as non-human.

A script configured to click every 500ms will fire events at t₀, t₀ + 500ms, t₀ + 1000ms. Heuristic detectors run Fast Fourier Transforms (FFT) or standard deviation checks on timestamp deltas (Δt). A standard deviation near zero (σ ≈ 0) is mathematical proof of software automation.

Human movement follows Fitts’s Law: the speed of the mouse slows down as it approaches the target bounding box. Teleporting the cursor or moving in perfectly straight lines with constant velocity is an immediate giveaway.

To defeat biometric behavioral filters, automation must reproduce mechanical human imperfections.

Instead of clicking a static target coordinate (X_target, Y_target), a human-simulated engine applies a randomized offset drawn from a normal (Gaussian) distribution:

Where δx and δy follow a Gaussian distribution N(0, σ²) bounded within [-jitter_max, +jitter_max].

For desktop automation, empirical testing shows that setting jitter_max = ±2px to ±3px matches natural human precision on 1080p and 4K displays while ensuring the click never misses clickable UI elements (buttons, checkboxes, claim faucets).

Similarly, delay intervals must not follow uniform random math. They must follow a biological latency curve with a positive skew:

This guarantees that while most actions occur around natural reaction speeds (250ms – 550ms), occasional longer hesitations (1200ms) naturally simulate human distraction or reading pauses.

The second greatest risk in desktop automation is Malware & Private Key Theft.

Many "free" macro tools circulating on Telegram channels and shady download sites are trojanized with info-stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar) designed to scan local directories for .json keystores and browser wallet extensions.

A secure automation workflow must obey three strict rules:

Rainbow Robotics, Neuromeka and Samhyun to exhibit Nov. 4â7 near Seoul; NVIDIA and Qualcomm to participate in conference

GOYANG, South Korea, Nov. 3, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- ROBOTWORLD 2026 will bring together 402 companies from 17 countries at the Korea International Exhibition Center (KINTEX) Nov. 4â7. The 1,198-booth exhibition will explore how Physical AI and AI-driven manufacturing systems are being developed for industrial applications.

International exhibitors will include Taiwan's Advantech, Japan's FANUC and China's Booster Robotics, PAXINI and Unitree. A Canadian delegation of 10 technology companies will participate in the exhibition and conference, while companies from India and Bangladesh will exhibit at ROBOTWORLD for the first time.

The International Robot Business Conference 2026 will extend the discussion of industrial applications through 36 sessions on Nov. 4â5. Its three tracks â Manufacturing AX (AI transformation), Humanoid with Physical AI and Mobility â will address AI adoption in production, robot intelligence and mobility across industrial and service environments. The Manufacturing AX track will feature presentations by representatives from Qualcomm and Advantech, while a speaker from NVIDIA will present in the Humanoid with Physical AI track. Qualcomm will also sponsor the exhibition.

According to the International Federation of Robotics' World Robotics 2026 report, global industrial robot installations reached a record 603,307 units in 2025, marking an 11% increase from the previous year and surpassing 600,000 units for the first time. Asia accounted for 76% of all newly installed industrial robots, with 457,315 units installed, up 14% year-on-year. Global installations are forecast to reach 655,000 units in 2026, reflecting the continued expansion of industrial automation.

Against this industrial backdrop, the exhibition will explore Physical AI technologies that enable robots to perceive their surroundings, assess conditions and perform physical tasks. Korean exhibitors will address both task intelligence and the platforms and components that enable movement and manipulation.

Rainbow Robotics will exhibit its RBY mobile humanoid and RBQ quadruped series, while Neuromeka will present its Physical Skill Foundation (PSF) technology alongside the EIR humanoid and NAMI mobile humanoid platforms. Neuromeka describes PSF as supporting task execution involving unfamiliar objects and changing work environments. Samhyun will showcase its AXLON actuator series for robot joint actuation, torque control and linear motion, together with the BOLDUM autonomous transport robot for industrial material handling with payloads of up to 3 metric tons.

Beyond individual robots, the exhibition will examine AI-driven manufacturing through systems that combine collaborative robots, autonomous mobile robots (AMRs), machine vision and manufacturing software. Exhibitors will demonstrate how process data and material flows can inform robot and equipment operations across production processes.

Humanoid, food-tech and healthcare pavilions will provide focused views of these technologies and their applications. The newly established humanoid pavilion will cover complete robots, actuators, reducers, sensors, controllers and AI software. The food-tech pavilion will feature cooking, serving and kitchen automation. The returning healthcare pavilion will cover surgical, rehabilitation, wearable and care robots, as well as hospital logistics.

Business programs will connect the technology presentations with opportunities for procurement, exports and investment. Export and procurement matchmaking sessions will run Nov. 4â6, with suppliers and prospective buyers matched in advance for one-to-one meetings. A seminar on entering international markets will be held on Nov.5 providing practical information on certification requirements in different countries.

Global Scale-up Day on Nov. 6 will be open to small and medium-sized enterprises as well as startups in AI and robotics seeking investment and growth capital. Conducted entirely in English, the program will bring together venture capital firms, accelerators, strategic investors and M&A specialists.

"With increased participation by global companies and the addition of national and special pavilions, we hope this year's exhibition will showcase the capabilities of Korean robotics companies and expand opportunities for practical cooperation between Korean and international businesses," a ROBOTWORLD representative said.

ROBOTWORLD 2026 will take place at KINTEX Exhibition Center 1, Halls 3â5, in Goyang. Information on exhibitors, conference sessions and business programs are available at https://eng.robotworld.or.kr/.

ROBOTWORLD is an international robotics exhibition covering industrial and service robots, core components, AI software and manufacturing automation solutions, alongside a conference and business programs. ROBOTWORLD 2026 is hosted by South Korea's Ministry of Trade, Industry and Resources (MOTIR) and jointly organized by the Korea Association of AI Robot Industry (KAR), the Korea Institute for Robot Industry Advancement (KIRIA) and the Institute of Control, Robotics and Systems (ICROS).

How does Oahu get to an economically resilient, 100% clean energy future?

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Oahu is a unique place. It’s the home of one of the largest clean tech incubators in the world, Elemental Impact, for a reason. Exorbitantly high electricity rates combined with ample natural resources make it an incredible laboratory to bring clean tech solutions to market. Rapid investment for WWII created a grid that was dependent on foreign oil. Path dependency (the idea that “what you know” becomes what you continue to do) hampered innovation and resulted in the creation of infrastructure (multiple power plants across multiple islands) that still, today, burns diesel fuel as large generators, to produce electricity for the island. Throw in a dependence on imported fuels that must come at least 3,000 miles to arrive, being the strategic hub for the entire Pacific theater of the U.S. military, one of the wildest weather years and hurricane seasons the island chain has ever seen AND the state’s economic dependency on tourism dollars, high cost of living, and low wages, and and wow – you’ve got yourself a puzzle and a half to solve in order to get the place to a sustainable future.

Analyst Mike Barnard loves a challenge, so he set forth to put together a roadmap to help Oahu get to 100% clean energy, which helps recirculate money in the state, lower costs, create jobs and foster economic resiliency.

Recently I gave a talk at Hawaii Tech Week in which I summarized this report, added in my own experience running an energy efficiency service that has now greened more than 20,000 homes and is saving Hawaii residents over $10 million a year on their utility bills, and then compiled the new and coming technologies that can help the island get off fossil fuels.

Mike’s report did something very important. The state’s own reporting and strategy looks at the big picture, a very difficult mountain to climb. Mike looked at that mountain and removed some of the false summits and unachievable viewpoints, resulting in a much more achievable and strategic pathway. What he did was eliminate the things we simply cannot fix right now – long distance flights, maritime bunkering, and military fuels – and tackled the problem of Oahu’s actual energy consumption for its residents. The result was that it was achievable, and there are technologies right now today that can be deployed in what he refers to as a “no regret stack” first and foremost.

I combined that with my approach, which was to showcase the economic resilience it creates. Case in point, an LED bulb saves so much money on Oahu that it pays for itself in 18 days. Every day after that, it is like change plinking in a piggy bank for the person who put it in. That builds economic resiliency for those who need it most. From LEDs to e-bikes to heat pump water heaters to balcony solar – it all adds up – fast payback periods, and financial independence.

Mike comes back to the things we simply can’t fix right now and argues that the tech will come – but for the duration of the 2020s and into the 2030s, we should be installing parking lot solar canopies, agrivoltaics, heat pump water heaters, and more.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

I Just Found Out What Christopher Nolan Made Universal’s Head Honcho Do When He Pitched The Odyssey

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The process of reading a Christopher Nolan script sounds like a spy thriller.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Director Christopher Nolan is the rare unicorn filmmaker who can draw audiences to the theater and bring in the kind of box office numbers that every major movie studio dreams of. Seeing a Nolan movie feels like special event viewing, partly because audiences go see his films with a level of anticipation and excitement that only comes with not having many details and never fully knowing what to expect. And, apparently, the head of Universal had an even more unique experience when Nolan pitched her The Odyssey.

There is a level of secrecy surrounding the Oppenheimer director’s projects, though I'm only now finding out that it carries over to even the Chairman of NBCUniversal Entertainment, Donna Langley. During an interview with Maria Sharapova for Pretty Tough, Universal’s Head Honcho described her experience with reading Christopher Nolan’s adaptation of The Odyssey, which sounds a bit like its own spy thriller. She revealed this about the IMAX savant's method, which includes his wife and producing partner, Emma Thomas:

It’s terrifying…. I’m terrified. Because well the way that Chris does it, or the way he did it on The Odyssey and on Oppenheimer, actually is I was invited over to their house, and I went by myself both occasions. And I didn't know what I was going to read because he doesn't say ahead of time. So you don't know until you're sitting in his library on the couch and he hands you the script. And so it’s a bit intimidating.

Not only does that sound intimidating and terrifying, I can’t imagine what would happen if you had a negative reaction while you were sitting in the home of the filmmaker responsible for writing it. Granted, Nolan is considered to be one of the best filmmakers in the entire world, but even the best at something has a bad day (or project) every once in a while, don’t they?

What strikes me as particularly interesting is the fact that even the biggest of big wigs at Universal doesn’t get to know ahead of time what she’s going to read when she knocks on Nolan’s door. I can’t imagine the level of heightened emotions that one experiences when reading a script from such an expert storyteller for the first time, as everything unfolds. But according to Langley, the pitch experience for this book-to-screen adaptation doesn’t just end there:

Then they leave, and I get a couple of hours to read the script on the same sofa…. And they leave me to it with a cup of tea. And then they leave, and then they come back when I'm finished and we talk about it.

It would probably be hilarious to watch the facial expressions of someone as they read a Christopher Nolan script. Especially someone so high up at the studio, as they realize what kind of budget they’ll need to do the story justice when it’s a story as epic as The Odyssey, one of the biggest blockbusters from the 2026 movie schedule. But knowing that, once finished, the Memento director and Thomas, will return to chat about it seems even more daunting. I wish I could have been a fly on the wall to hear everything that they talked about once Donna Langley finished reading the Odyssey script. Langley added:

It’s a process that — I love it, because it feels very old school, and it’s very intentional. And it makes you pay attention, but it is slightly intimidating. But I think, in the best way possible, it makes me feel very alive.

I have to admit, there is something really cool about making everyone involved with getting a movie into production feel like they’re a part of a private little club of people let in on a secret that the world is not yet ready for. It’s an old-school approach, keeping technology out of it, and that tracks for Nolan, given he doesn't even own a smartphone. However, I understand how exhilarating it must be to be a part of the cool club. And Langley said that the scripts for The Odyssey and Oppenheimer were both brilliant and easy to talk about once she finished them.

The Odyssey's reviews were largely positive from general audiences and critics alike, even if some did point out historical inaccuracies and that side character problem. This historical epic lit up the 2026 box office in a big way, proving that if you release a movie people want to see, they will go see it, likely over and over again. Now, I'm just wondering when Nolan and Thomas might reach out to Langley with another script that would lead them to repeat their unique process.

AMD beats expectations as agentic AI sparks its data center revenue growth engines

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shares of the chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices Inc. were up more than 5% in late trading today after it delivered solid third-quarter earnings and revenue today, with data center sales almost doubling from a year ago.

The company reported earnings before certain costs such as stock compensation of $2.09 per share, easily beating Wall Street’s forecast of $1.95 per share. Revenue for the period jumped 45% from a year earlier to $13.38 billion, ahead of the $13.09 billion analyst consensus estimate. All told, the company’s net income for the quarter came to $3.47 billion, up 77% from the previous year when it posted a profit of just $1.96 billion.

The star performer in the quarter was AMD’s data center business unit, which accounted for 64% of its overall revenue, up from just 47% one year ago. After spending years trying to compete with Nvidia Corp.’s graphics processing units for artificial intelligence market share, the company’s traditional central processing units or CPUs are suddenly back in vogue thanks to the emergence of agentic AI applications such as Meta Platforms Inc.’s Muse. Autonomous AI agents can run much more efficiently on CPUs than they can on GPUs, boosting demand for the chips.

Looking ahead, AMD expects its server revenue to double year-over-year in fiscal 2027, with AI growth likely to accelerate even faster than the company projected just three months ago. “Adoption is just starting and we expect demand to grow sharply as agents are deployed more broadly and take on more complex work,” said AMD Chief Executive Lisa Su (pictured) on a conference call with analysts.

AMD’s data center story isn’t all about CPUs, though. The company also sells GPUs, including bundles of them shipped inside data center-ready rack-scale systems called Helios, and that system’s popularity is growing fast. Su said Anthropic PBC, OpenAI Group PBC, Microsoft Corp., Oracle Corp. and Meta are all using AMD Helios systems in their data centers. Last week, Meta said its first Helion racks are already up and running as it revealed that AMD invested $1 billion in its latest equity offering.

The chipmaker has also committed to a $5 billion investment in Anthropic, which last week revealed the long-awaited prospectus for its upcoming initial public offering.

AMD delivered its latest earnings report just days after its rival Intel Corp. did the same. Intel also posted strong data center revenue growth, but its stock fell more than 30% on Friday due to weak guidance and concerns over its ability to meet demand for its products. But Su said AMD had no such problems. She made a high-profile visit to Taiwan last month where she held meetings with Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. and a number of supply chain partners, and said after the trip that the company plans to substantially increase its supply in the coming year. “We have worked across our supply chain to secure the wafers, substrates, advanced packaging, memory and other key components needed to support much higher growth next year,” Su added.

AMD’s results will go some way towards reassuring investors who have become increasingly nervous about the direction of the AI industry and whether or not the hundreds of billions of dollars being spent on it each year is actually going to generate a return on their investments, said Emarketer analyst Jacob Bourne. “The results are a test of AMD’s own performance and the broader AI trade, and its data center and revenue beats will encourage investors after the recent semiconductor selloff,” he added.

While AMD is far from immune to the recent volatility that has afflicted AI-related stocks, its future still looks increasingly rosy for now. “Demand for AI computing is not going to fall off a cliff, and AMD has the competitive products and major customer relationships to capitalize on that demand,” Bourne said.

Strength in the data center unit is helping AMD to ride out difficulties in its traditional market for personal computers, which is the reason for Intel’s poor outlook. During the second quarter, global PC shipments were down 4% from a year earlier, with buyers dissuaded by the rising cost of components such as memory chips, according to data from Counterpoint Research. Along with Intel, AMD is a leading manufacturer of the x86 CPUs that power most of the world’s computers and laptops. The company reports PC chip revenue within a segment that also includes its chips for games consoles, and that unit saw its revenue decline 8% from a year earlier to $3.7 billion. Gaming revenue was down 40% from the previous year.

For the current quarter, AMD is targeting revenue of around $17 billion at the midpoint of its guidance range, ahead of the Street’s $16.2 billion consensus estimate. The company also sees its gross margins coming in at 55%, just below the Street’s forecast of 55.3%.

AMD was busy on the acquisition front during the quarter. In September it revealed it’s going to buy World Labs Inc. for $8.2 billion in an all-stock deal. The San Francisco-based AI lab was founded by the AI industry luminary Fei-Fei Li, and has developed world models that can simulate 3D environments to support the training of intelligent robots and other physical AI applications.

The company also acquired a startup called Taalas Inc. that makes specialized model-specific integrated circuits, which are essentially chips that can be customized to run a specific AI model more efficiently. That acquisition should help to boost AMD’s position in AI inference. AMD also invested in the AI image generation model maker Stability AI Ltd., which raised $76 million in funding in August.

Blumhouse’s New Hit R-Rated Horror Movie Officially Sets Digital Release Date

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Blumhouse's new R-rated horror movie has been a box office smash hit, going on to gross $108 million worldwide after being made on a reported production budget between $18 and $19 million. Jessica Chastain plays the dual roles of mother Ursula Martin and an evil entity who looks like her known as Other Mommy. Ursula attempts to protect her daughter from this nefarious force in the adaptation of Josh Malerman's 2024 novel Incidents Around the House.

Now, distributor Universal Pictures has announced that Other Mommy will be available to watch on digital platforms starting on November 10, including on Prime Video and Fandango at Home. About a month and a half later, on December 29, Universal Pictures Home Entertainment will release the movie in 4K and on Blu-ray.

When purchasing the horror film at participating retailers or buying it as physical media, there will be exclusive bonus content, including deleted and alternate scenes, behind-the-scenes looks at the special effects and performances, and the cast and filmmakers discussing the subversion of horror tropes. Check out the bonus features below:

When the trailer started playing ahead of The Odyssey in July, Other Mommy quickly gained attention for the jump scare that happens when the titular character appears in a contorted position underneath a table. This set the stage for the box office success that occurred following the movie's theatrical debut on October 9.

Despite being a financial hit, the reviews have been largely negative. The film holds a 19% score from critics on Rotten Tomatoes based on 100 reviews, along with a 48% audience score based on more than 1,000 verified ratings. In ScreenRant's Other Mommy review, Josh Bate gave the movie four out of 10 stars and wrote that "the audience immediately has all the answers to the film’s story, even before they’ve met a single character . . . Nothing is scary about Other Mommy, and only the film’s very first sequence is even slightly creepy."

Chastain's performance is one area of the movie that has been praised, including the critics' consensus on Rotten Tomatoes declaring that "despite a valiant turn from Jessica Chastain as the spectral embodiment of a smothering parent, Other Mommy proves too silly for its intermittently effective jump scares to add up to a spooky whole."

For those who may have been dissuaded from seeing the movie in theaters due to the bad reviews, the digital release now provides an opportunity to watch at home through rental or purchase. A streaming release date has not been set yet, but when it is, the film is expected to go to Peacock since it is owned by NBCUniversal.

Other Mommy was directed by Rob Savage, with a script written by Nathan Elston, James Wan producing, and Malerman as an executive producer alongside Michael Clear, Judson Scott, and Ryan Lewis. In addition to Chastain and Clark, the cast includes Jay Duplass, Dichen Lachman, Arian Moayed, Karen Allen, Sean Kaufman, Adam Silver, and Alec Secăreanu.

Other Mommy makes its digital debut on November 10.

How Oregon State turned a raw athlete into a starting linebacker

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CORVALLIS — Two years ago, Jarrel Comer was a freshman at Tallahassee Community College and had not played competitive football since middle school. Now he's a starting outside linebacker at Oregon State.

Comer played basketball at Eastside High School in Gainesville, Florida, but began college as a regular student. He looked for opportunities to play sports again and football became the opportunity at Citrus College in California.

He boarded a plane for the first time to take a cross-country flight to play football for the first time in more than four years.

"I was willing to take that chance to get to where I am today," Comer said.

He had 58 tackles (8.5 for loss), including six sacks at the California junior college and got on the radar of linebackers coach Cort Dennison, then at Idaho. Dennison kept pursuing Comer at OSU, where he signed in January and arrived during the spring.

Justin Joyner wins first game as Oregon State coach in blowout

Atkinson, Legree join Nike's Blue Ribbon Elite as OSU extends apparel deal

Pac-12 confirms play clock error led to penalty on Oregon State in its loss to Fresno State

At 6-foot-4 and 245 pounds, Comer was dominating in junior college from his athleticism alone. He didn't know the finer points of playing football. Hand placement, setting the edge in the run game and pass coverage were not in his repertoire. Comer was there to pin his ears back and attack the quarterback, regardless of the down and distance.

"They didn't ask much of me," Comer said. "They told me just do what I do, use my athleticism, get to the quarterback."

What Comer lacks in experience, he makes up for in ability. He consistently ranks among OSU's fastest players in practice, which was invaluable to a team seeking more pass rush.

"His athleticism is off the charts," defensive coordinator Mike MacIntyre said. "We see him grow leaps and bounds. Playing a lot more (against Montana), he will grow leaps and bounds going into this Saturday because now he's seen it, it slows down, he understands the different things."

RELATED: Experts release Oregon State vs. UTEP predictions, best bets, and preview for Week 4

Comer has 10 tackles thus far, including four in each of the past two games, and is already more than a situational pass rusher for the Beavers. He credits MacIntyre and edge coaches Joe Seumalo and George Frazier with helping him learn the game, particularly pass coverage.

Ideally, pairing Comer with Takari Hickle will allow OSU to generate more pressure as teams won't be able to focus all their offensive line on one side.

"Teams have to respect his speed," Hickle said. "If they don't, then he'll run right by them. He is definitely a threat in coverage and in the pass game."

BigBear.ai vs. Nebius: Which AI Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of these AI stocks is shrinking and losing money, while the other is growing at triple digits and just turned profitable.

As the artificial intelligence (AI) race intensifies, investors are looking beyond the biggest names for hidden opportunities. Deciding between BigBear.ai (BBAI -1.94%) and Nebius Group (NBIS +7.25%) requires weighing government contracts against cloud infrastructure.

BigBear.ai focuses on decision-intelligence software for high-stakes defense environments. Nebius builds the actual full-stack infrastructure needed to train and deploy these complex models. While both companies operate in the fast-moving AI sector, their business models, financial health, and customer bases offer investors starkly different paths.

BigBear.ai provides specialized decision-intelligence and autonomy software tailored for government and commercial sectors. It provides biometric and data analysis solutions, with a major concentration of contracts adding a layer of risk.

On a trailing-12-month (TTM) basis through the June quarter, revenue reached nearly $142 million, which represents a decline of approximately 1.5% compared to the prior year.

The company reported a net loss of close to $180 million. This performance indicates that expenses significantly exceeded total sales.

As of its June 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 4.9x, measuring the ability to cover short-term debts with current assets. The debt-to-equity ratio was 0, as the company carries no debt.

TTM free cash flow, which is cash from operations minus capital expenditures, was negative $73 million, indicating the business consumes more cash than it generates.

Nebius Group positions itself as an AI cloud company, building the full-stack infrastructure required for complex machine learning tasks. Its platform supports AI builders across healthcare and financial services by offering tools for model training and deployment. The company operates a global footprint with data centers and roughly 400 AI engineers to maintain its competitive position.

As of the June quarter, TTM revenue reached nearly $1.4 billion, representing a significant 444% year-over-year increase. Net income improved to $62 million, up from under $10 million for the full year 2025. This performance shows a swing to profitability following significant net losses in previous years, driven by high demand for cloud services.

Based on its June 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 4.0x, while the debt-to-equity ratio is roughly 1.0x. The company's total debt was approximately $8.6 billion, with an additional $1.5 billion in lease liabilities. This was partially offset by $8 billion of cash and equivalents.

TTM free cash flow was negative $5.9 billion, reflecting the capital-intensive nature of the company's AI infrastructure. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 21.6% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation because it is a non-cash expense.

BigBear.ai faces significant risks from revenue concentration, as a large portion of its business depends on a limited number of government contracts. These agreements are subject to budgetary fluctuations and may be terminated at any time for convenience.

Nebius is funding massive capital spending needs. Heavy capital investment for data centers has resulted in negative free cash flow, and a slowdown in AI demand remains the primary risk.

BigBear.ai appears cheaper based on sales over the past twelve months, while Nebius is the only one with a positive forward P/E using future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

I would buy Nebius over BigBear right now. BigBear has a multi-year history of flat revenue. It reported $146 million in revenue in 2021, and $128 million in 2025. It has reported a net loss across that period. The company has to compete for government contracts, while Nebius has seen its revenue explode due to a shortage of data center capacity and high demand.

Nebius is seeing immediate demand for its infrastructure as soon as it is brought online. Additionally, Nebius benefits from pricing power, with recent bids for compute capacity fetching higher prices than before.

Analysts expect BigBear to continue reporting losses over the next few years, while expecting Nebius to continue growing earnings over the long term as it brings more capacity online.

The risk for Nebius is that the stock trades at high multiples of sales and earnings. A lot of growth is priced in, so anything that stalls the AI build-out could hurt the stock. The stock has already fallen 38% in the last month over concerns about AI spending.

Still, Nebius has a scarce asset that is in high demand, and that scarcity is being validated by explosive revenue growth and improving profitability. That's the stock I would rather hold for 2027 and beyond.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
