Alaska judge finds Bonanza Channel gold mining proposal unlawful

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tribal villages successfully questioned the legality of corporate plans to dredge for gold in Safety Sound and the Bonanza Channel.

ANCHORAGE, Alaska (CN) â An estuary abundant with wildlife in northwestern Alaska will likely be spared from an industrial suction dredging operation, though a federal judge Tuesday refrained from making the final call for now.

U.S. District Judge Sharon L. Gleason, a Barack Obama appointee, ruled the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers unlawfully approved a Nevada companyâs gold mining project plan in Safety Sound and its Bonanza Channel, located east of Nome.

The five Alaska Native tribal groups who sued the Corps, including the Village of Solomon and the King Island Native Community, had asked Gleason to vacate the Corpsâ 2024 approval. Instead, the judge asked them and the agency to provide additional briefing within three weeks on what a court-imposed remedy should look like.

The Bonanza Channel is renowned for birdwatching, having received official recognition as an area of importance by Audubon and the American Bird Conservatory. Itâs also home to fish and marine mammal species, all contributing to an ecosystem that has supported Alaska Native subsistence living for countless generations.

Plans by Las Vegas-registered IPOP LLC to mine the estuary date back to 2018, when the corporation said it would bundle a reality television show, âRivers of Gold," with the mineral operation.

IPOP and its president, Beau Epstein, have since dropped that concept, but the mining plan, which entails vacuuming the estuary bed, removing more than 4 million cubic yards of material over five years and significantly altering sections of terrain and vegetation, has hit legal roadblocks at multiple points. In 2022, the Corpsâ Alaska District rejected IPOPâs Clean Water Act permit request for the proposal after concluding that Safety Sound waters would face significant degradation threats.

In 2023, however, Corps official Kirk Gibbs vacated that permit denial after IPOP accused the Alaska District of being biased towards Alaska Native interests. The permit review was assigned to the Hawaii-based Pacific Division instead, which approved the plan.

The Pacific Division opted not to make an environmental impactsStatement for the proposal, as the Corps often does for large-scale resource development projects, writing instead that the IPOP projectâs impacts âare expected to be not significant and adverse.â

In her ruling, Gleason repeatedly drew from the Alaska Districtâs nearly 120-page explanation for denying the permit in 2022, rebuffing the Pacific Divisionâs approval as inconsistent with environmental law.

âThe division did not explain why it disagreed with the districtâs conclusion that impacts to birds and other wildlife would be major or why, in its view, the project constituted only a small part of the estuary system such that any impact would be minor and temporary,â Gleason wrote.

After submitting supplemental briefs to Gleason by Nov. 24, the Alaska Native plaintiffs and Pacific Division will be given another two weeks to respond to each other.

âItâs frustrating that our communities have to continually defend our lands, our waters, and our way of life against decisions that fail to recognize just how much is at stake for our people,â said Deilah Johnson, tribal resources director for the Village of Solomon. âThese arenât just environmental concerns to us. This is our food security, our traditions, and the future of our children. We shouldnât have to fight this hard to be heard, and we certainly shouldnât have to wait until the damage is done for our concerns to matter. This ruling is an important victory, but our work isnât finished. We will continue to stand together and fight for the protection our communities deserve.â

IPOP LLC did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Oregon wildfire victims defend jury instruction in billion-dollar PacifiCorp suit

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The fires over Labor Day weekend in 2020 displaced thousands of Oregon residents.

PORTLAND, Ore. (CN) â Victims of the destructive series of wildfires that ravaged Oregon over Labor Day weekend in 2020 implored the state Supreme Court on Tuesday to revive a jury verdict in their favor.

In one of the state’s largest disasters, fires blazed across Oregon during a historic windstorm in September 2020. PacifiCorp and Pacific Power had left their power lines energized, and those energy lines ignited the flames.

Thousands of survivors of four fires â the 242 Fire, the Echo Mountain Complex Fire, the South Obenchain Fire, and the Santiam Canyon Fire â sued the power companies, accusing them of failing to reasonably maintain the power lines, clear vegetation surrounding the lines and protect against fire. They also claimed the companies negligently failed to shut down power to the lines despite warnings.

The trial court certified a class of fire survivors harmed by the four fires and structured the trial so the jury would determine liability and causation for each fire. After an eight-week trial, the jury found PacifiCorp caused the fires and harmed class members. Nineteen damages-only trials then produced verdicts and judgments exceeding $1 billion.

However, PacifiCorp appealed, challenging an instruction that told the jury the class representatives brought evidence on behalf of absent class members and jurors could âassume that the evidence at trial applies to all class members.â

The Oregon Court of Appeals sided with PacifiCorp, holding that common evidence didnât prove causation and the jury may have assumed evidence of liability for one fire meant liability for the others.

Before the Supreme Court Tuesday, the fire survivors â a class of over 2,400 individuals â argued the appeals court made a mistake.

âAt the end of the day, what they really don’t like is the jury verdict in this case,â said Nicholas Rosinia, attorney representing the class of wildfire survivors said, referring to the power companies. âThey bet the company on a class action wildfire trial, and they lost.â

âHere, the jury decided the case on a fire area by fire area basis," Rosinia continued. “That’s not to say the evidence was not common across the class as a whole, but it permitted, in this case, PacifiCorp to make fire-specific defenses to the extent it had fire-specific defenses.”

The fire survivors argue the jury simply needed to determine that PacifiCorpâs fires were a substantial factor in the resulting harm.

âThe PacifiCorp’s conflagration of fires was the common evidence that proved the ultimate harm, and that’s why all of the class members relied on that same body of evidence with respect to the conflagration to prove their case,â Rosinia said.

The wildfire survivors said there was no dispute that there was harm across the class area.

âThe only dispute was whether PacifiCorp’s conflagration of fires was a substantial factor, and the evidence that everybody relied on was common as to that question,â Rosinia said.

PacifiCorp disagreed, arguing the class action was unprecedented, that the class should not have been certified, and that the instruction invited the jury to assume the evidence presented at trial applied to every single class member.

âFor the jury, class actions are hard enough, but they’re told this is one big class. This wasn’t subclasses by fire areas â one big class, 17 class representatives, they widely differ among them, and the jury is told, âYou can consider all of the evidence as applying to all the class members,â" said Theodore Boutrous, attorney for PacifiCorp. âI’ve never seen anything like it.â

Oregon Supreme Court Justice Christopher Garrett questioned PacifiCorpâs theory.

âIt appears just from reading the briefs and the verdict form that the case was pretty clearly tried on a fire-by-fire basis and the verdict form reflects that,â Garrett remarked. âSo how real is the worry that the jury would have transposed causation from fire number one to fire number three?â

PacifiCorp argued the worry is quite real, given the sheer size of the class and varying circumstances.

âDo we really think the jury would think that a fire burning a hundred miles away proved a different fire?â questioned Oregon Supreme Court Justice Meagan Flynn.

PacifiCorp contended that yes, the jury very well could have thought so. It also argued the trial court should not have certified such a large class.

âThere hasn’t been a class action certified anywhere in the country with one wildfire. Here, we’ve got four fire areas, and there were dozens of fires within those fires,â Boutrous said. âIt was just a fundamental error to certify one big class with 17 representatives who themselves couldn’t have been in a class just alone because they were in completely different places.â

The Oregon Supreme Court did not indicate when it would rule.

Brazil expands federal powers over public security

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The constitutional amendment passed without opposition.

RIO DE JANEIRO (CN) â Brazil approved a constitutional amendment Tuesday that expands the federal governmentâs role in public security, directs oil revenue to policing and prisons and calls for tougher penalties against criminal organizations.

The Senate approved President Luiz InÃ¡cio Lula da Silvaâs proposal in two rounds of voting, with no opposing votes, after separating out amendments that would lower the age of criminal responsibility. The lower house approved the amendment March 4, clearing the way for Congress to formally enact it without presidential approval.

The vote marks a victory for Lula after a presidential campaign in which public security figured prominently. The amendment also clears the way for a Public Security Ministry, whose creation Lula had made contingent on its approval.

The government secured approval before the new Congress takes office in 2027, when the opposition will have greater strength in the Senate.

Lula welcomed the vote in a social media post, saying the new ministry would focus on intelligence, planning and cooperation with states and municipalities to confront criminal factions and militias.

âProtection for citizens. Prison for criminals,â Lula wrote. âThe Brazilian people have the right to work, return home and live in peace. Guaranteeing that right is a commitment of Brazilâs government.â

JoÃ£o Gabriel Madeira Pontes, an attorney who holds a doctorate in public law from Rio de Janeiro State University, welcomed greater federal involvement and cooperation among different levels of government.

âUnder the original framework of the 1988 Constitution, public security was almost exclusively the responsibility of the states,â Pontes said.

The amendment adds the Unified Public Security System, which already exists under federal law, to the Constitution. It provides for joint operations, information sharing and integration of federal, state and municipal agenciesâ systems.

It restricts the governmentâs ability to freeze money in federal public security and prison funds or use it for other purposes, while directing 10% of the surplus in the oil-funded Social Fund to those programs. The transfers will begin in 2027 and reach the full percentage in 2029.

The text requires legislation establishing tougher penalties for members of criminal organizations based on their rank within those groups. These include incarceration in maximum-security or other specialized facilities, limits or bans on moving to less restrictive prison regimes and confiscation of assets linked to criminal activities.

The amendment also expands the Federal Policeâs authority to investigate militias and criminal organizations operating across state or international borders. The Federal Highway Police will be able to patrol federal railways and waterways in addition to highways.

Municipalities will be able to establish civilian police forces for patrols and community policing, provided they meet requirements for training, funding and coordination with other forces. Public prosecutors will exercise external oversight.

Pontes said the main challenge will be integrating new federal and municipal responsibilities with existing state police structures. The amendment maintains the division between military police, responsible for patrols, and civil police, responsible for criminal investigations.

âHow will this fit together with an old institutional structure that has been criticized for many years â the statesâ public security structure?â Pontes said. âI think there will be considerable difficulty integrating these changes with the institutional framework that already existed at the state level.â

Pontes also questioned the effectiveness of relying on incarceration, which he described as âsomewhat the logic behind the amendment.â

âMass incarceration has always been a phenomenon in Brazil, and it grows every year. And we havenât achieved much through incarceration. I really donât know how this could improve public security, specifically the fight against organized crime,â Pontes said.

A dispute over the age of criminal responsibility, currently 18, had threatened to delay approval. The opposition proposed lowering it to 16 generally and 14 for certain crimes, while a Workersâ Party proposal would lower it to 16 only for crimes classified as heinous under Brazilian law. Senators voted 38-36 to consider those proposals separately, avoiding another vote on the amendment in the lower house.

Senator FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro, whom Lula defeated in the presidential election, participated remotely from the United States and voted against considering the proposals separately. He left Brazil on Oct. 27, after a Supreme Court justice ordered him to surrender his passports as part of an investigation into suspected money laundering.

Courthouse News reporter MarÃ­lia Marasciulo is based in Brazil.

Environmentalists successfully block California’s first carbon capture project

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

TerraVault was one oil and gas company's plan to inject and store up to 2 million tons of carbon dioxide underground every year.

(CN) âÂ A Superior Court judge ruled Tuesday that Kern County’s approval of California’s first carbon capture and storage project violated the state’s bedrock environmental law, the California Environmental Quality Act.

The ruling is a win for local environmental justice and conservation groups, including the Sierra Club and the Center for Biological Diversity, who argued the county had left “multiple environmental, public health and safety issues” unaddressed when it approved the landmark project.

âFor years, Kern County residents have raised serious concerns about increased pollution and the safety of millions of tons of CO2 stored beneath their community," Ileana Navarro of the Central California Environmental Justice Network said in a written statement. “We are so grateful that the court recognized what Kern County failed to see and gave community concerns the serious consideration they deserve.”

Located at the Elk Hills Oil Field in the San Joaquin Valley and built by the California Resources Corporation, the state’s largest oil and gas company, TerraVault began capturing carbon dioxide from oil wells and injecting it underground in May. Once completed, the two vaults, made from depleted oil reservoirs, are expected to capture more than 2 million tons of carbon dioxide every year âÂ the equivalent of what more than 400,000 cars would emit annually.

In an email, President and CEO of the California Resources Corporation Francisco Leon expressed disappointment in the ruling, writing: “This project is the result of years of work, including close consultation with surrounding communities where we received overwhelming support for our efforts. This order is a step in the wrong direction for addressing the impacts of climate change and reflects the weaponization of CEQA by a narrow group of special interests. We are still evaluating the order and will aggressively pursue all available options to protect and advance this important project.â

Some environmental groups have said TerraVault I will, in effect, extend the life of the Elk Hills Oil Field, the seventh most productive in the U.S. The California Resources Corporation has said it could also store carbon from a range of not-yet-built factories in the region, which could produce, among other things, cement and steel. These new industries, opponents say, would further pollute a low-income area already struggling with bad air quality.

In their CEQA lawsuit, the plaintiffs argued the environmental impact report, prepared by the California Resources Corporation and approved by Kern County, didn’t properly account for the eight possible industries TerraVault I could capture carbon for, even though the new factories were a “reasonably foreseeable consequence” of the project. Superior Court Judge Marcos Camacho agreed.

“The record evidence shows that all CO2 capture equipment, transportation pipelines, compression facilities, injection and monitoring wells, and underground pore space exist solely to receive and store CO2,” Camacho wrote in the ruling. “Thus, the source facilities and industries unquestionably change the scope and nature of the project… The Court finds that such omission indicates that the whole of the project was not considered, in violation of CEQA.”

The judge found plenty of other shortcomings in the environmental report, referred to in the ruling as the REIR.Â “The issue of atmospheric leakage of CO2,” for example, was not “fully addressed.” He also found the report “fails to adequately mitigate the project’s greenhouse gas impacts.”

“This is a lack of analysis in the REIR, not a disagreement among experts,” Camacho wrote.

The ruling means TerraVault must temporarily shut down. The California Resources Corporation will have to prepare a new environmental impact report, a long and costly process, before it can restart operations.

The fight over TerraVault exposes a deep and long-standing divide within the environmental movement. Some groups believe climate change is the biggest threat to life on earth, and to avoid the most dire consequences, we must employ any and all tactics to remove carbon from the atmosphere. One report by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change reads, âThe deployment of [carbon dioxide removal] to counterbalance hard-to-abate residual emissions is unavoidable if net zero CO2 or GHG emissions are to be achieved."

Other groups, including the plaintiffs suing to stop TerraVault, say the way to stop climate change is to cut emissions.

“California needs to invest in real solutions like clean wind and solar to solve our energy and climate emergencies,” said Michelle Ghafar, an attorney with Earthjustice that represented the plaintiffs, in a written statement. “We canât waste time on âHail Maryâ projects pushed by the fossil fuel industry that will only delay our progress and further entrench pollution in frontline communities.â

Why Cancer Biomarker Testing Is Personal for Mark-Paul Gosselaar

📅 Nov 02, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Known for his iconic roles on “Saved by the Bell,” “NYPD Blue,” “Franklin & Bash,” and “Found,” Mark-Paul Gosselaar knows how to grab an audience.

Now, he’s using that star power to raise awareness about cancer biomarker testing while sharing how he prioritizes his own health and well-being.

“Health is a huge thing for my wife and I, and our family,” Gosselaar told Healthline. “We are constantly aware of new technologies.”

Having seen people in his life navigate cancer diagnoses, Gosselaar is now raising awareness about the role of biomarker testing in cancer treatment through his partnership with AstraZeneca and its Meet Your Mark campaign.

The campaign aims to educate people facing cancer diagnoses and their loved ones about how biomarker testing may help doctors make more informed, personalized treatment decisions. It also encourages patients to discuss testing with their healthcare teams.

“Biomarkers may tell doctors the specific type of cancer present and if targeted therapies or immunotherapy might help,” Mark Socinski, MD, oncologist and Executive Medical Director of the AdventHealth Cancer Institute, told Healthline.

He explained that biomarkers are typically genes or proteins that doctors measure through blood or tissue tests. They are used to help guide treatment in numerous diseases but are especially powerful tools in many types of cancer.

“They help decode why cancers behave differently, even when two people have the same cancer type,” said Socinski. “However, while biomarker testing provides useful information, it does not guarantee a specific outcome.”

Despite those limitations, Socinski sees promise in the shift from one-size-fits-all cancer treatment to care tailored to each patient’s specific cancer characteristics.

“Biomarker testing does play a critical role here, and it is important for people to be aware of the potential benefits,” Socinski said. “It may help change the course of a person’s treatment journey.”

Gosselaar hopes that greater awareness of biomarker testing can help people facing a cancer diagnosis feel more informed about their treatment options.

“A lot of the time, when a loved one or someone who has been diagnosed with cancer [it can] feel like your world is in a tailspin,” he said. “I wanted to get the word out for anyone who’s going through a cancer diagnosis, or a caretaker, to know that biomarker testing is an option, because even though two cancers are similar, their tumors can act very differently and biomarker testing may help the medical expert with more personalized treatment.”

As part of the campaign, Gosselaar took part in a commercial, something he hadn’t done since before his days on “Saved by the Bell.”

“This was the first time that I actually had to be on set and not be a character; I had to be me talking about this very serious issue,” he said.

While it was a challenging experience, he said it was also fulfilling.

“I enjoyed the process, and to be able to use my platform to be able to spread the word; it means a lot to me,” he said. “I’m very selective of the things that I want to attach myself to, and this seemed like the right fit.”

He particularly enjoyed recreating Zack Morris’s signature “timeout” gesture from “Saved by the Bell,” which allowed his character to freeze time and speak directly to the camera.

“Using something from the past that was such a symbolic gesture to so many people of my generation that are now my age and possibly going through [cancer] or knowing someone who’s going through [cancer to] direct them to [this campaign], means a lot,” he said.

Beyond his efforts to raise awareness about cancer biomarker testing, Gosselaar is equally focused on taking care of his own health.

Getting quality sleep is one of his top priorities. He sleeps on a high-tech mattress that monitors sleep stages, heart rate, heart rate variability, and respiratory rate. He also uses a biometric health and fitness tracker, prioritizes healthy eating, and uses saunas and red light therapy.

“We do everything that we possibly can, but I’m less about longevity and more about just living a healthy lifestyle,” Gosselaar said. “I want to continue doing the things that I’ve done since I was a child…without feeling held back by my health.”

As he embraces his 50s, he said he’d like to live a long life for his children and future grandchildren. He started taking his health seriously in his 40s after undergoing shoulder surgery.

“[It] really made me realize [that] all the damage I had done in my 20s and 30s sort of adds up later in life, and that’s probably something that I would tell the younger me… to preserve a little bit more and take [an] approach that recovery is just as important as the workout because I did not use my recovery very well; it was not a priority for me,” said Gosselaar.

He also makes time for himself every day. He said doing so makes him a better person and more able to care for his wife and four children.

“[It’s] something that I believe in; that I need to do something for myself before I can do something for someone else. Or if I do something for someone else, then I have to equalize that by doing something for me,” he said.

Daily physical activity also helps support his mental well-being.

“I need to do something where I challenge myself, and it could be as simple as a walk. It could be as simple as just getting outside and lifting something heavy [or] taking bags of groceries and taking the long way into your house,” Gosselaar said.

“Like challenging yourself with picking up things, you know, taking backpacks from the car from your kids and seeing if you can carry three in one hand…or going into the gym and spending an hour in the gym, or 30 minutes, or just moving.”

When he’s in the sauna, he works on breathing and clearing his thoughts.

“Some people need to meditate, some people need to talk to somebody. I encourage all of that because I think all of that helps,” he said.

“I’ve done them all. I’ve done the cold plunging, I’ve done all that stuff…some of it works, and some of it doesn’t; it’s whatever you believe in. So I just encourage people to stay healthy and move, get outside, and help other people.”
